arXiv:2107.00793v3 [cs.LG] 03 Oct 2022

The Causal-Neural Connection:
Expressiveness, Learnability, and Inference

Kevin Xia Affiliation: CausalAI Lab Affiliation: Columbia University Email: kmx2000@columbia.edu   
Kai-Zhan Lee Affiliation: Bloomberg L.P. Affiliation: Columbia University Email: 
kl2792@columbia.edu    Yoshua Bengio Affiliation: MILA Affiliation: Université de Montréal Email: 
yoshua.bengio@mila.quebec    Elias Bareinboim Affiliation: CausalAI Lab Affiliation: Columbia
University Email: eb@cs.columbia.edu

Abstract

One of the central elements of any causal inference is an object called structural causal model
(SCM), which represents a collection of mechanisms and exogenous sources of random variation of the
system under investigation (Pearl, 2000). An important property of many kinds of neural networks is
universal approximability: the ability to approximate any function to arbitrary precision. Given
this property, one may be tempted to surmise that a collection of neural nets is capable of
learning any SCM by training on data generated by that SCM. In this paper, we show this is not the
case by disentangling the notions of expressivity and learnability. Specifically, we show that the
causal hierarchy theorem (Thm. 1, Bareinboim et al., 2020), which describes the limits of what can
be learned from data, still holds for neural models. For instance, an arbitrarily complex and
expressive neural net is unable to predict the effects of interventions given observational data
alone. Given this result, we introduce a special type of SCM called a neural causal model (NCM),
and formalize a new type of inductive bias to encode structural constraints necessary for
performing causal inferences. Building on this new class of models, we focus on solving two
canonical tasks found in the literature known as causal identification and estimation. Leveraging
the neural toolbox, we develop an algorithm that is both sufficient and necessary to determine
whether a causal effect can be learned from data (i.e., causal identifiability); it then estimates
the effect whenever identifiability holds (causal estimation). Simulations corroborate the proposed
approach.

1 Introduction

One of the most celebrated and relied upon results in the science of intelligence is the
universality of neural models. More formally, universality says that neural models can approximate
any function (e.g., boolean, classification boundaries, continuous valued) with arbitrary precision
given enough capacity in terms of the depth and breadth of the network (Cybenko,, 1989; Hornik,,
1991; Leshno et al.,, 1993; Lu et al.,, 2017). This result, combined with the observation that most
tasks can be abstracted away and modeled as input/output – i.e., as functions – leads to the
strongly held belief that under the right conditions, neural networks can solve the most
challenging and interesting tasks in AI. This belief is not without merits, and is corroborated by
ample evidence of practical successes, including in compelling tasks in computer vision (Krizhevsky
et al.,, 2012), speech recognition (Graves and Jaitly,, 2014), and game playing (Mnih et al.,, 2013
). Given that the universality of neural nets is such a compelling proposition, we investigate this
belief in the context of causal reasoning.

To start understanding the causal-neural connection – i.e., the non-trivial and somewhat intricate
relationship between these modes of reasoning – two standard objects in causal analysis will be
instrumental. First, we evoke a class of generative models known as the Structural Causal Model
(SCM, for short) (Pearl,, 2000, Ch. 7). In words, an SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} is a representation of a
system that includes a collection of mechanisms and a probability distribution over the exogenous
conditions (to be formally defined later on). Second, any fully specified SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}
induces a collection of distributions known as the Pearl Causal Hierarchy (PCH) (Bareinboim et
al.,, 2020, Def. 9). The importance of the PCH is that it formally delimits distinct cognitive
capabilities (also known as layers; not to be confused with neural nets layers) that can be
associated with the human activities of “seeing” (layer 1), “doing” (2), and “imagining” (3) (Pearl
and Mackenzie,, 2018, Ch. 1). ^1^1 1 This structure is named after Judea Pearl and is a central
topic in his Book of Why (BoW), where it is also called the “Ladder of Causation” (Pearl and
Mackenzie,, 2018). For a more technical discussion on the PCH, we refer readers to (Bareinboim et
al.,, 2020). Each of these layers can be expressed as a distinct formal language and represents
queries that can help to classify different types of inferences (Bareinboim et al.,, 2020, Def. 8).
Together, these layers form a strict containment hierarchy (Bareinboim et al.,, 2020, Thm. 1). We
illustrate these notions in Fig. 1(a) (left side), where SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} induces layers
L1∗,L2∗,L3∗L^{*}_{1},L^{*}_{2},L^{*}_{3} of the PCH.

(a)UnobservedNature/Truth(b)Learned/HypothesizedPCH:Structural CausalModel ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}ℒ1∗\
mathcal{L}_{1}^{*}ℒ2∗\mathcal{L}_{2}^{*}ℒ3∗\mathcal{L}_{3}^{*}NeuralModel 𝒩\mathcal{N}ℒ1\mathcal{L}
_{1}ℒ2\mathcal{L}_{2}ℒ3\mathcal{L}_{3}Training (ℒ1∗=ℒ1\mathcal{L}_{1}^{*}=\mathcal{L}_{1})
Figure 1: The l.h.s. contains the unobserved true SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} that induces the three
layers of the PCH. The r.h.s. contains an NCM that is trained to match in layer 1. The matching
shading indicates that the two models agree w.r.t. L1L_{1} while not necessarily agreeing w.r.t.
layers 2 and 3.

Even though each possible statement within these capabilities has well-defined semantics given the
true SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} (Pearl,, 2000, Ch. 7), a challenging inferential task arises when one
wishes to recover part of the PCH when ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} is only partially observed. This situation
is typical in the real world aside from some special settings in physics and chemistry where the
laws of nature are understood with high precision.

For concreteness, consider the setting where one needs to make a statement about the effect of a
new intervention (i.e., about layer 2), but only has observational data from layer 1, which is
passively collected.^2^2 2 The full inferential challenge is, in practice, more general since an
agent may be able to perform interventions and obtain samples from a subset of the PCH’s layers,
while its goal is to make inferences about some other parts of the layers (Bareinboim and Pearl,,
2016; Lee et al.,, 2019; Bareinboim et al.,, 2020). This situation is not uncommon in RL settings
Sutton and Barto, (2018); Forney et al., (2017); Lee and Bareinboim, (2018); Lee and Bareinboim,
(2020). Still, for the sake of space and concreteness, we will focus on two canonical and more
basic tasks found in the literature. Going back to the causal-neural connection, one could try to
learn a neural model 𝒩\mathcal{N} using the observational dataset (layer 1) generated by the true
SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}, as illustrated in Fig. 1(b). Naturally, a basic consistency requirement is
that 𝒩\mathcal{N} should be capable of generating the same distributions as ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}; in
this case, their layer 1 predictions should match (i.e., L1=L1∗L_{1}=L^{*}_{1}). Given the
universality of neural models, it is not hard to believe that these constraints can be satisfied in
the large sample limit. The question arises of whether the learned model 𝒩\mathcal{N} can act as a
proxy, having the capability of predicting the effect of interventions that matches the L2L_{2}
distribution generated by the true (unobserved) SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}. ^3^3 3 We defer a more
formal discussion on how neural models could be used to assess the effect of interventions to Sec.
2. Still, this is neither attainable in all universal neural architectures nor trivially
implementable. The answer to this question cannot be ascertained in general, as will become evident
later on (Corol. 1). The intuitive reason behind this result is that there are multiple neural
models that are equally consistent w.r.t. the L1L_{1} distribution of ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} but
generate different L2L_{2}-distributions. ^4^4 4 Pearl shared a similar observation in the BoW (
Pearl and Mackenzie,, 2018, p. 32): “Without the causal model, we could not go from rung (layer)
one to rung (layer) two. This is why deep-learning systems (as long as they use only rung-one data
and do not have a causal model) will never be able to answer questions about interventions (…)”. 
Even though 𝒩\mathcal{N} may be expressive enough to fully represent ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} (as
discussed later on), generating one particular parametrization of 𝒩\mathcal{N} consistent with L1L_
{1} is insufficient to provide any guarantee regarding higher-layer inferences, i.e., about
predicting the effects of interventions (L2L_{2}) or counterfactuals (L3L_{3}).

The discussion above entails two tasks that have been acknowledged in the literature, namely,
causal effect identification and estimation. The first – causal identification – has been
extensively studied, and general solutions have been developed, such as Pearl’s celebrated
do-calculus (Pearl,, 1995). Given the impossibility described above, the ingredient shared across
current non-neural solutions is to represent assumptions about the unknown ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} in the
form of causal diagrams (Pearl,, 2000; Spirtes et al.,, 2000; Bareinboim and Pearl,, 2016) or their
equivalence classes (Jaber et al.,, 2018; Perković et al.,, 2018; Jaber et al.,, 2019; Zhang,, 2008
). The task is then to decide whether there is a unique solution for the causal query based on such
assumptions. There are no neural methods today focused on solving this task.

The second task – causal estimation – is triggered when effects are determined to be identifiable
by the first task. Whenever identifiability is obtained through the backdoor criterion/conditional
ignorability (Pearl,, 2000, Sec. 3.3.1), deep learning techniques can be leveraged to estimate such
effects with impressive practical performance (Shalit et al.,, 2017; Louizos et al.,, 2017; Li and
Fu,, 2017; Johansson et al.,, 2016; Yao et al.,, 2018; Yoon et al.,, 2018; Kallus,, 2020; Shi et
al.,, 2019; Du et al.,, 2021; Guo et al.,, 2020; Kennedy et al.,, 2021; Johansson et al.,, 2021).
For effects that are identifiable through causal functionals that are not necessarily of the
backdoor-form (e.g., frontdoor, napkin), other optimization/statistical techniques can be employed
that enjoy properties such as double robustness and debiasedness Jung et al., 2020a (); Jung et
al., 2020b (); Jung et al., (2021). Each of these approaches optimizes a particular estimand
corresponding to one specific target interventional distribution.

Despite all the great progress achieved so far, it is still largely unknown how to perform the
tasks of causal identification and estimation in arbitrary settings using neural networks as a
generative model, acting as a proxy for the true SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}. It is our goal here to
develop a general causal-neural framework that has the potential to scale to real-world,
high-dimensional domains while preserving the validity of its inferences, as in traditional
symbolic approaches. In the same way that the causal diagram encodes the assumptions necessary for
the do-calculus to decide whether a certain query is identifiable, our method encodes the same
invariances as an inductive bias while being amenable to gradient-based optimization, allowing us
to perform both tasks in an integrated fashion (in a way, addressing Pearl’s concerns alluded to in
Footnote 4). Specifically, our contributions are as follows:

 1. 1.
   
    [[Sec. 2]] We introduce a special yet simple type of SCM that is amenable to gradient descent
    called a neural causal model (NCM). We prove basic properties of this class of models,
    including its universal expressiveness and ability to encode an inductive bias representing
    certain structural invariances (Thm. 1-3). Notably, we show that despite the NCM’s
    expressivity, it still abides by the Causal Hierarchy Theorem (Corol. 1).

 2. 2.
   
    [[Sec. 3]] We formalize the problem of neural identification (Def. 8) and prove a duality
    between identification in causal diagrams and in neural causal models (Thm. 4). We introduce an
    operational way to perform inferences in NCMs (Corol. 2-3) and a sound and complete algorithm
    to jointly train and decide effect identifiability for an NCM (Alg. 1, Corol. 4).

 3. 3.
   
    [[Sec. 4]] Building on these results, we develop a gradient descent algorithm to jointly
    identify and estimate causal effects (Alg. 2).

There are multiple ways of grounding these theoretical results. In Sec. 5, we perform experiments
with one possible implementation which support the feasibility of the proposed approach. All
appendices including proofs, experimental details, and examples can be found in the full technical
report (Xia et al.,, 2021).

1.1 Preliminaries

In this section, we provide the necessary background to understand this work, following the
presentation in (Pearl,, 2000). An uppercase letter XX indicates a random variable, and a lowercase
letter xx indicates its corresponding value; bold uppercase 𝐗\mathbf{X} denotes a set of random
variables, and lowercase letter 𝐱\mathbf{x} its corresponding values. We use 𝒟X\mathcal{D}_{X} to
denote the domain of XX and 𝒟𝐗=𝒟X1×⋯×𝒟Xk\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{X}}=\mathcal{D}_{X_{1}}\times\dots\
times\mathcal{D}_{X_{k}} for 𝐗={X1,…,Xk}\mathbf{X}=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\}. We denote P⁡(𝐗)P(\mathbf
{X}) as a probability distribution over a set of random variables 𝐗\mathbf{X} and P⁡(𝐗=𝐱)P(\mathbf
{X}=\mathbf{x}) as the probability of 𝐗\mathbf{X} being equal to the value of 𝐱\mathbf{x} under the
distribution P⁡(𝐗)P(\mathbf{X}). For simplicity, we will mostly abbreviate P⁡(𝐗=𝐱)P(\mathbf{X}=\
mathbf{x}) as simply P⁡(𝐱)P(\mathbf{x}). The basic semantic framework of our analysis rests on 
structural causal models (SCMs) (Pearl,, 2000, Ch. 7), which are defined below.

1Structural Causal Model (SCM).

An SCM ℳ\mathcal{M} is a 4-tuple ⟨𝐔,𝐕,ℱ,P⁡(𝐔)⟩\langle\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V},\mathcal{F},P(\mathbf
{U})\rangle, where 𝐔\mathbf{U} is a set of exogenous variables (or “latents”) that are determined
by factors outside the model; 𝐕\mathbf{V} is a set {V1,V2,…,Vn}\{V_{1},V_{2},\ldots,V_{n}\} of
(endogenous) variables of interest that are determined by other variables in the model – that is,
in 𝐔∪𝐕\mathbf{U}\cup\mathbf{V}; ℱ\mathcal{F} is a set of functions {fV1,fV2,…,fVn}\{f_{V_{1}},f_{V_
{2}},\ldots,f_{V_{n}}\} such that each fif_{i} is a mapping from (the respective domains of)
𝐔Vi∪𝐏𝐚Vi\mathbf{U}_{V_{i}}\cup\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{i}} to ViV_{i}, where 𝐔Vi⊆𝐔\mathbf{U}_{V_{i}}\
subseteq\mathbf{U}, 𝐏𝐚Vi⊆𝐕∖Vi\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{i}}\subseteq\mathbf{V}\setminus V_{i}, and the entire
set ℱ\mathcal{F} forms a mapping from 𝐔\mathbf{U} to 𝐕\mathbf{V}. That is, for i=1,…,ni=1,\ldots,n,
each fi∈ℱf_{i}\in\mathcal{F} is such that vi←fVi​(𝐩𝐚Vi,𝐮Vi)v_{i}\leftarrow f_{V_{i}}(\mathbf{pa}_
{V_{i}},\mathbf{u}_{V_{i}}); and P⁡(𝐮)P(\mathbf{u}) is a probability function defined over the
domain of 𝐔\mathbf{U}. ■\blacksquare

Each SCM ℳ\mathcal{M} induces a causal diagram GG where every Vi∈𝐕V_{i}\in\mathbf{V} is a vertex,
there is a directed arrow (Vj→Vi)(V_{j}\rightarrow V_{i}) for every Vi∈𝐕V_{i}\in\mathbf{V} and
Vj∈P​a​(Vi)V_{j}\in Pa(V_{i}), and there is a dashed-bidirected arrow (Vj⇠⇢Vi)(V_{j}\dashleftarrow\
dashrightarrow V_{i}) for every pair Vi,Vj∈𝐕V_{i},V_{j}\in\mathbf{V} such that 𝐔Vi\mathbf{U}_{V_
{i}} and 𝐔Vj\mathbf{U}_{V_{j}} are not independent. For further details on this construction, see (
Bareinboim et al.,, 2020, Def. 13/16, Thm. 4). The exogenous 𝐔Vi\mathbf{U}_{V_{i}}’s are not
assumed independent (i.e. Markovianity does not hold). We will consider here recursive SCMs, which
implies acyclic diagrams, and that the endogenous variables (𝐕\mathbf{V}) are discrete and have
finite domains.

We show next how an SCM ℳ\mathcal{M} gives values to the PCH’s layers; for details on the
semantics, see (Bareinboim et al.,, 2020, Sec. 1.2). Superscripts are omitted when unambiguous.

2Layers 1, 2 Valuations.

An SCM ℳ\mathcal{M} induces layer L2​(ℳ)L_{2}(\mathcal{M}), a set of distributions over 𝐕\mathbf
{V}, one for each intervention 𝐱\mathbf{x}. For each 𝐘⊆𝐕{\mathbf{Y}}\subseteq\mathbf{V},

 mathbf{u}\mid{\mathbf{Y}}_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{u})={\mathbf{y}}\}}P(\mathbf{u}),

where 𝐘𝐱​(𝐮){\mathbf{Y}}_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{u}) is the solution for 𝐘\mathbf{Y} after evaluating
ℱ𝐱:={fVi:Vi∈𝐕∖𝐗}∪{fX←x:X∈𝐗}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{x}}:=\{f_{V_{i}}:V_{i}\in\mathbf{V}\setminus\mathbf
{X}\}\cup\{f_{X}\leftarrow x:X\in\mathbf{X}\}.
The specific distribution P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}), where 𝐗\mathbf{X} is empty, is defined as layer L1​
(ℳ)L_{1}(\mathcal{M}). ■\blacksquare

In words, an external intervention forcing a set of variables 𝐗\mathbf{X} to take values 𝐱\mathbf
{x} is modeled by replacing the original mechanism fXf_{X} for each X∈𝐗X\in\mathbf{X} with its
corresponding value in 𝐱\mathbf{x}. This operation is represented formally by the do-operator, d​o​
(𝐗=𝐱)do(\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{x}), and graphically as the mutilation procedure. For the definition of
the third layer, L3​(ℳ)L_{3}(\mathcal{M}), see Def. 9 in Appendix A or (Bareinboim et al.,, 2020,
Def. 7).

2 Neural Causal Models and the Causal Hierarchy Theorem

In this section, we aim to resolve the tension between expressiveness and learnability (Fig. 1). To
that end, we define a special class of SCMs based on neural nets that is amenable to optimization
and has the potential to act as a proxy for the true, unobserved SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}.

3NCM.

A Neural Causal Model (for short, NCM) M^​(𝛉)\widehat{M}(\bm{\theta}) over variables 𝐕\mathbf{V}
with parameters 𝛉={θVi:Vi∈𝐕}\bm{\theta}=\{\theta_{V_{i}}:V_{i}\in\mathbf{V}\} is an SCM ⟨𝐔^,𝐕,ℱ^,P⁡
(𝐔^)⟩\langle\widehat{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{V},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},P(\widehat{\mathbf{U}})\rangle
such that

  • •
   
    𝐔^⊆{U^𝐂:𝐂⊆𝐕}\widehat{\mathbf{U}}\subseteq\{\widehat{U}_{\mathbf{C}}:\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf
    {V}\}, where each U^\widehat{U} is associated with some subset of variables 𝐂⊆𝐕\mathbf{C}\
    subseteq\mathbf{V}, and 𝒟U^=[0,1]\mathcal{D}_{\widehat{U}}=[0,1] for all U^∈𝐔^\widehat{U}\in\
    widehat{\mathbf{U}}. (Unobserved confounding is present whenever |𝐂|>1|\mathbf{C}|>1.)

  • •
   
    ℱ^={f^Vi:Vi∈𝐕}\widehat{\mathcal{F}}=\{\hat{f}_{V_{i}}:V_{i}\in\mathbf{V}\}, where each f^Vi\hat
    {f}_{V_{i}} is a feedforward neural network parameterized by θVi∈𝜽\theta_{V_{i}}\in\bm{\theta}
    mapping values of 𝐔Vi∪𝐏𝐚Vi\mathbf{U}_{V_{i}}\cup\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{i}} to values of ViV_{i} for
    some 𝐏𝐚Vi⊆𝐕\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{i}}\subseteq\mathbf{V} and 𝐔Vi={U^𝐂:U^𝐂∈𝐔^,Vi∈𝐂}\mathbf{U}_{V_{i}}=\
    {\widehat{U}_{\mathbf{C}}:\widehat{U}_{\mathbf{C}}\in\widehat{\mathbf{U}},V_{i}\in\mathbf{C}\};

  • •
   
    P⁡(𝐔^)P(\widehat{\mathbf{U}}) is defined s.t. U^∼Unif⁡(0,1)\widehat{U}\sim\unif(0,1) for each U
    ^∈𝐔^\widehat{U}\in\widehat{\mathbf{U}}.

■\blacksquare

There is a number of remarks worth making at this point.

 1. 1.
   
    [Relationship NCM →\rightarrow SCM] By definition, all NCMs are SCMs, which means NCMs have the
    capability of generating any distribution associated with the PCH’s layers.

 2. 2.
   
    [Relationship SCM ↛\nrightarrow NCM] On the other hand, not all SCMs are NCMs, since Def. 3
    dictates that 𝐔^\widehat{\mathbf{U}} follows uniform distributions in the unit interval and ℱ^\
    widehat{\mathcal{F}} are feedforward neural networks.

 3. 3.
   
    [Non-Markovianity] For any two endogenous variables ViV_{i} and VjV_{j}, it is the case that
    𝐔Vi\mathbf{U}_{V_{i}} and 𝐔Vj\mathbf{U}_{V_{j}} might share an input from 𝐔^\widehat{\mathbf
    {U}}, which will play a critical role in causality, not ruling out a priori the possibility of
    unobserved confounding and violations of Markovianity.

 4. 4.
   
    [Universality of Feedforward Nets] Feedforward networks are universal approximators Cybenko,
    (1989); Hornik, (1991) (see also Goodfellow et al., (2016)), and any probability distribution
    can be generated by the uniform one (e.g., see probability integral transform Angus, (1994)).
    This suggests that the pair ⟨ℱ^,P⁡(𝐔^)⟩\langle\widehat{\mathcal{F}},P(\widehat{\mathbf{U}})\
    rangle may be expressive enough for modeling ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}’s mechanisms ℱ\mathcal{F} and
    distribution P⁡(𝐔)P(\mathbf{U}) without loss of generality.

 5. 5.
   
    [Generalizations / Other Model Classes] The particular modeling choices within the definition
    above were made for the sake of explanation, and the results discussed here still hold for
    other, arbitrary classes of functions and probability distributions, as shown in Appendix D.

To compare the expressiveness of NCMs and SCMs, we introduce the following definition.

4P(Li){}^{(L_{i})}-Consistency.

Consider two SCMs, ℳ1\mathcal{M}_{1} and ℳ2\mathcal{M}_{2}. ℳ2\mathcal{M}_{2} is said to be P(Li){}
^{(L_{i})}-consistent (for short, LiL_{i}-consistent) w.r.t. ℳ1\mathcal{M}_{1} if Li​(ℳ1)=Li​(ℳ2)L_
{i}(\mathcal{M}_{1})=L_{i}(\mathcal{M}_{2}). ■\blacksquare

This definition applies to NCMs since they are also SCMs. As shown below, NCMs can not only
approximate the collection of functions of the true SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}, but they can perfectly 
represent all the observational, interventional, and counterfactual distributions. This property
is, in fact, special and not enjoyed by many neural models. (For examples and discussion, see
Appendix C and D.1.)

1NCM Expressiveness.

For any SCM ℳ∗=⟨𝐔,𝐕,ℱ,P⁡(𝐔)⟩\mathcal{M}^{*}=\langle\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V},\mathcal{F},P(\mathbf{U})\
rangle, there exists an NCM M^​(𝛉)=⟨𝐔^,𝐕,ℱ^,P⁡(𝐔^)⟩\widehat{M}(\bm{\theta})=\langle\widehat{\mathbf
{U}},\mathbf{V},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},P(\widehat{\mathbf{U}})\rangle s.t. M^\widehat{M} is L3L_{3}
-consistent w.r.t. ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}. ■\blacksquare

Thm. 1 ascertains that there is no loss of expressive power using NCMs despite the constraints
imposed over its form, i.e., NCMs are as expressive as SCMs. One might be tempted to surmise,
therefore, that an NCM can be trained on the observed data and act as a proxy for the true SCM ℳ∗\
mathcal{M}^{*}, and inferences about other quantities of ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} can be done through
computation directly in ℳ^\widehat{\mathcal{M}}. Unfortunately, this is almost never the case: ^5^5
5 Multiple examples of this phenomenon are discussed in Appendix C.1 and (Bareinboim et al.,, 2020,
Sec. 1.2)

1Neural Causal Hierarchy Theorem (N-CHT).

Let Ω∗\Omega^{*} and Ω\Omega be the sets of all SCMs and NCMs, respectively. We say that Layer jj
of the causal hierarchy for NCMs collapses to Layer ii (i<ji<j) relative to ℳ∗∈Ω∗\mathcal{M}^{*}\in
\Omega^{*} if Li​(ℳ∗)=Li​(M^)L_{i}(\mathcal{M}^{*})=L_{i}(\widehat{M}) implies that Lj​(ℳ∗)=Lj​(M^)
L_{j}(\mathcal{M}^{*})=L_{j}(\widehat{M}) for all M^∈Ω\widehat{M}\in\Omega. Then, with respect to
the Lebesgue measure over (a suitable encoding of L3L_{3}-equivalence classes of) SCMs, the subset
in which Layer jj of NCMs collapses to Layer ii has measure zero. ■\blacksquare

This corollary highlights the fundamental challenge of performing inferences across the PCH layers
even when the target object (NCM ℳ^\widehat{\mathcal{M}}) is a suitable surrogate for the
underlying SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}, in terms of expressiveness and capability of generating the same
observed distribution. That is, expressiveness does not mean that the learned object has the same
empirical content as the generating model. For concrete examples of the expressiveness of NCMs and
why it is insufficient for causal inference, see Examples 1 and 2 in Appendix C.1. Thus, structural
assumptions are necessary to perform causal inferences when using NCMs, despite their
expressiveness. We discuss next how to incorporate the necessary assumptions into an NCM to
circumvent the limitation highlighted by Corol. 1.

2.1 A Family of Neural-Interventional Constraints (Inductive Bias)

In this section, we investigate constraints about ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} that will narrow down the
hypothesis space and possibly allow for valid cross-layer inferences. One well-studied family of
structural constraints comes in the form of a pair comprised of a collection of interventional
distributions 𝒫\mathcal{P} and causal diagram 𝒢\mathcal{G}, known as a causal bayesian network
(CBN) (Def. 15; see also (Bareinboim et al.,, 2020, Thm. 4))). The diagram 𝒢\mathcal{G} encodes
constraints over the space of interventional distributions 𝒫\mathcal{P} which are useful to perform
cross-layer inferences (for details, see Appendix C.2). For simplicity, we focus on interventional
inferences from observational data. To compare the constraints entailed by distinct SCMs, we define
the following notion of consistency:

(a)UnobservedNature/Truth(b)Learned/HypothesizedPCH:SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}=⟨ℱ∗,P⁡(𝐔∗)⟩=\langle\
mathcal{F}^{*},P(\mathbf{U}^{*})\rangleℒ1∗\mathcal{L}_{1}^{*}ℒ2∗\mathcal{L}_{2}^{*}ℒ3∗\mathcal{L}_
{3}^{*}NCM M^\widehat{M}=⟨ℱ^,P⁡(𝐔^)⟩=\langle\widehat{\mathcal{F}},P(\widehat{\mathbf{U}})\rangleℒ1\
mathcal{L}_{1}ℒ2\mathcal{L}_{2}ℒ3\mathcal{L}_{3}Training (ℒ1∗=ℒ1\mathcal{L}_{1}^{*}=\mathcal{L}_
{1})CausalDiagram𝒢\mathcal{G}𝒢\mathcal{G}-Constraint
Figure 2: The l.h.s. contains the true SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} that induces PCH’s three layers. The
r.h.s. contains an NCM that is trained with layer 1 data. The matching shading indicates that the
two models agree with respect to L1L_{1} while not necessarily agreeing in layers 2 and 3. The
causal diagram 𝒢\mathcal{G} entailed by ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} is used as an inductive bias for M^\
widehat{M}.

5𝒢\mathcal{G}-Consistency.

Let 𝒢\mathcal{G} be the causal diagram induced by SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}. For any SCM ℳ\mathcal{M},
we say that ℳ\mathcal{M} is 𝒢\mathcal{G}-consistent (w.r.t. ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}) if 𝒢\mathcal{G} is a
CBN for L2​(ℳ)L_{2}(\mathcal{M}). ■\blacksquare

In the context of NCMs, this means that ℳ\mathcal{M} would impose the same constraints over 𝒫\
mathcal{P} as the true SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} (since 𝒢\mathcal{G} is also a CBN for L2​(ℳ∗)L_{2}(\
mathcal{M}^{*}) by (Bareinboim et al.,, 2020, Thm. 4)). Whenever the corresponding diagram 𝒢\
mathcal{G} is known, one should only consider NCMs that are 𝒢\mathcal{G}-consistent. ^6^6 6
Otherwise, the causal diagram can be learned through structural learning algorithms from
observational data Spirtes et al., (2000); Peters et al., (2017) or experimental data Kocaoglu et
al., 2017a (); Kocaoglu et al., (2019); Jaber et al., (2020). See the next footnote for a neural
take on this task. We provide below a systematic way of constructing 𝒢\mathcal{G}-consistent NCMs.

6C2C^{2}-Component.

For a causal diagram 𝒢\mathcal{G}, a subset 𝐂⊆𝐕\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{V} is a complete
confounded component (for short, C2C^{2}-component) if any pair Vi,Vj∈𝐂V_{i},V_{j}\in\mathbf{C} is
connected with a bidirected arrow in 𝒢\mathcal{G} and is maximal (i.e. there is no C2C^{2}
-component 𝐂′\mathbf{C}^{\prime} for which 𝐂⊂𝐂′\mathbf{C}\subset\mathbf{C}^{\prime}.) ■\blacksquare

7𝒢\mathcal{G}-Constrained NCM (constructive).

Let 𝒢\mathcal{G} be the causal diagram induced by SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}. Construct NCM M^\widehat
{M} as follows. (1) Choose 𝐔^\widehat{\mathbf{U}} s.t. U^𝐂∈𝐔^\widehat{U}_{\mathbf{C}}\in\widehat{\
mathbf{U}} if and only if 𝐂\mathbf{C} is a C2C^{2}-component in 𝒢\mathcal{G}. (2) For each variable
Vi∈𝐕V_{i}\in\mathbf{V}, choose 𝐏𝐚Vi⊆𝐕\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{i}}\subseteq\mathbf{V} s.t. for every Vj∈𝐕V_
{j}\in\mathbf{V}, Vj∈𝐏𝐚ViV_{j}\in\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{i}} if and only if there is a directed edge from
VjV_{j} to ViV_{i} in 𝒢\mathcal{G}. Any NCM in this family is said to be 𝒢\mathcal{G}-constrained.
■\blacksquare

Note that this represents a family of NCMs, not a unique one, since 𝜽\bm{\theta} (the parameters of
the neural networks) are not yet specified by the construction, only the scope of the function and
independence relations among the sources of randomness (𝐔^\widehat{\mathbf{U}}). In contrast to
SCMs where both ⟨ℱ,P⁡(𝐮)⟩\langle\mathcal{F},P(\mathbf{u})\rangle can freely vary, the degrees of
freedom within NCMs come from 𝜽\bm{\theta}. ^7^7 7 There is a growing literature that models SCMs
using neural nets as functions, but which differ in nature and scope to our work. Broadly, these
works assume Markovianity, which entails strong constraints over P⁡(𝐔)P(\mathbf{U}) and, in the
context of identification, implies that all effects are always identifiable; see Corol. 3. For
instance, (Goudet et al.,, 2018) attempts to learn the entire SCM from observational (L1L_{1})
data, while (Bengio et al.,, 2020; Brouillard et al.,, 2020) also leverages experimental (L2L_{2})
data. On the inference side, (Kocaoglu et al., 2017b, ) focuses on estimating causal effects of
labels on images.

We show next that an NCM constructed following the procedure dictated by Def. 7 encodes all the
constraints of the original causal diagram.

2NCM 𝒢\mathcal{G}-Consistency.

Any 𝒢\mathcal{G}-constrained NCM M^​(𝛉)\widehat{M}(\bm{\theta}) is 𝒢\mathcal{G}-consistent. ■\
blacksquare

We show next the implications of imposing the structural constraints embedded in the causal
diagram.

3L2L_{2}-𝒢\mathcal{G} Representation.

For any SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} that induces causal diagram 𝒢\mathcal{G}, there exists a 𝒢\mathcal{G}
-constrained NCM M^​(𝛉)=⟨𝐔^,𝐕,ℱ^,P⁡(𝐔^)⟩\widehat{M}(\bm{\theta})=\langle\widehat{\mathbf{U}},\
mathbf{V},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},P(\widehat{\mathbf{U}})\rangle that is L2L_{2}-consistent w.r.t. ℳ∗
\mathcal{M}^{*}. ■\blacksquare

The importance of this result stems from the fact that despite constraining the space of NCMs to
those compatible with 𝒢\mathcal{G}, the resultant family is still expressive enough to represent
the entire Layer 2 of the original, unobserved SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}.

Fig. 2 provides a mental picture useful to understand the results discussed so far. The true SCM ℳ∗
\mathcal{M}^{*} generates the three layers of the causal hierarchy (left side), but in many
settings only observational data (layer 1) is visible. An NCM M^\widehat{M} trained with this data
is capable of perfectly representing L1L_{1} (right side). For almost any generating ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^
{*} sampled from the space Ω∗\Omega^{*}, there exists an NCM M^\widehat{M} that exhibits the same
behavior with respect to observational data (M^\widehat{M} is L1L_{1}-consistent) but exhibits a
different behavior with respect to interventional data. In other words, L1L_{1} underdetermines
L2L_{2}. (Similarly, L1L_{1} and L2L_{2} underdetermine L3L_{3} (Bareinboim et al.,, 2020, Sec.
1.3).) Still, the true SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} also induces a causal diagram 𝒢\mathcal{G} that
encodes constraints over the interventional distributions. If we use this collection of constraints
as an inductive bias, imposing GG-consistency in the construction of the NCM, M^\widehat{M} may
agree with those of the true ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} under some conditions, which we will investigate in
the next section.

3 The Neural Identification Problem

We now investigate the feasibility of causal inferences in the class of 𝒢\mathcal{G}-constrained 
NCMs. ^8^8 8 This is akin to what happens with the non-neural CHT (Bareinboim et al.,, 2020, Thm.
1) and the subsequent use of causal diagrams to encode the necessary inductive bias, and in which
the do-calculus allows for cross-layer inferences directly from the graphical representation (
Bareinboim et al.,, 2020, Sec. 1.4). The first step is to refine the notion of identification (
Pearl,, 2000, pp. 67) to inferences within this class of models.

8Neural Effect Identification.

Consider any arbitrary SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} and the corresponding causal diagram 𝒢\mathcal{G} and
observational distribution P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}). The causal effect P⁡(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))P(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\
mathbf{x})) is said to be neural-identifiable from the set of 𝒢\mathcal{G}-constrained NCMs Ω⁡(𝒢)\
Omega(\mathcal{G}) and observational distribution P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}) if and only if PM^1​(𝐲∣d​o​
(𝐱))=PM^2​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))P^{\widehat{M}_{1}}(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x}))=P^{\widehat{M}_{2}}(\mathbf
{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x})) for every pair of models M^1,M^2∈Ω⁡(𝒢)\widehat{M}_{1},\widehat{M}_{2}\in\
Omega(\mathcal{G}) s.t. Pℳ∗​(𝐕)=PM^1​(𝐕)=PM^2​(𝐕)>0P^{\mathcal{M}^{*}}(\mathbf{V})=P^{\widehat{M}_
{1}}(\mathbf{V})=P^{\widehat{M}_{2}}(\mathbf{V})>0. ■\blacksquare

Figure 3: P⁡(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))P(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x})) is identifiable from P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}) and
Ω⁡(𝒢)\Omega(\mathcal{G}) if for any SCM ℳ∗∈Ω∗\mathcal{M}^{*}\in\Omega^{*} and NCMs M^1,M^2∈Ω\

{x})) (bottom right).

In the context of graphical identifiability (Pearl,, 2000, Def. 3.2.4) and do-calculus, an effect
is identifiable if any SCM in Ω∗\Omega^{*} compatible with the observed causal diagram and capable
of generating the observational distribution matches the interventional query. If we constrain our
attention to NCMs, identification in the general class would imply identification in NCMs,
naturally, since it needs to hold for all SCMs. On the other hand, it may be insufficient to
constrain identification within the NCM class, like in Def. 8, since it is conceivable that the
effect could match within the class (perhaps in a not very expressive neural architecture) while
there still exists an SCM that generates the same observational distribution and induces the same
diagram, but does not agree in the interventional query; see Example 7 in Appendix C. The next
result shows that this is never the case with NCMs, and there is no loss of generality when
deciding identification through the NCM class.

4Graphical-Neural Equivalence (Dual ID).

Let Ω∗\Omega^{*} be the set of all SCMs and Ω\Omega the set of NCMs. Consider the true SCM ℳ∗\
mathcal{M}^{*} and the corresponding causal diagram 𝒢\mathcal{G}. Let Q=P⁡(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))Q=P(\mathbf{y}
\mid do(\mathbf{x})) be the query of interest and P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}) the observational
distribution. Then, QQ is neural identifiable from Ω⁡(𝒢)\Omega(\mathcal{G}) and P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V})
if and only if it is identifiable from 𝒢\mathcal{G} and P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}). ■\blacksquare

In words, Theorem 4 relates the solution space of these two classes of models, which means that the
identification status of a query is preserved across settings. For instance, if an effect is
identifiable from the combination of a causal graph 𝒢\mathcal{G} and P⁡(𝐯)P(\mathbf{v}), it will
also be identifiable from 𝒢\mathcal{G}-constrained NCMs (and the other way around). This is
encouraging since our goal is to perform inferences directly through neural causal models, within
Ω⁡(𝒢)\Omega(\mathcal{G}), avoiding the symbolic nature of do-calculus computation; the theorem
guarantees that this is achievable in principle.

2Neural Mutilation (Operational ID).

Consider the true SCM ℳ∗∈Ω∗\mathcal{M}^{*}\in\Omega^{*}, causal diagram 𝒢\mathcal{G}, the
observational distribution P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}), and a target query QQ equal to Pℳ∗​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))P^{\
mathcal{M}^{*}}(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x})). Let M^∈Ω⁡(𝒢)\widehat{M}\in\Omega(\mathcal{G}) be a
𝒢\mathcal{G}-constrained NCM that is L1L_{1}-consistent with ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}. If QQ is
identifiable from 𝒢\mathcal{G} and P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}), then QQ is computable through a mutilation
process on a proxy NCM M^\widehat{M}, i.e., for each X∈𝐗X\in\mathbf{X}, replacing the equation fxf_
{x} with a constant xx (Q=proc-mutilation​(M^;𝐗=𝐱,𝐘𝐶𝐿𝑂𝑆𝐸Q=\textsc{proc-mutilation}(\widehat{M};\
mathbf{X}=\mathbf{x},\mathbf{Y})). ■\blacksquare

Following the duality stated by Thm. 4, this result provides a practical, operational way of
evaluating queries in NCMs: inferences may be carried out through the process of mutilation, which
gives semantics to queries in the generating SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} (via Def. 2). What is
interesting here is that the proposition provides conditions under which this process leads to
valid inferences, even when ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} is unknown, or when the mechanisms ℱ\mathcal{F} and
exogenous distribution P⁡(𝐔)P(\mathbf{U}) of ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} and the corresponding functions and
distribution of the proxy NCM M^\widehat{M} do not match. (For concreteness, refer to example 5 in
Appendix. C.) In words, inferences using mutilation on M^\widehat{M} would work as if they were on
ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} itself, and they would be correct so long as certain stringent properties were
satisfied – L1L_{1}-consistency, 𝒢\mathcal{G}-constraint, and identifiability. As shown earlier, if
these properties are not satisfied, inferences within a proxy model will almost never be valid,
likely bearing no relationship with the ground truth. (For fully worked out instances of this
situation, refer to examples 2, 3, or 4 in Appendix C).

Still, one special class of SCMs in which any interventional distribution is identifiable is called
Markovian, where all UiU_{i} are assumed independent and affect only one endogenous variable ViV_
{i}.

3Markovian Identification.

Whenever the 𝒢\mathcal{G}-constrained NCM ℳ^\widehat{\mathcal{M}} is Markovian, P⁡(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))P(\
mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x})) is always identifiable through the process of mutilation in the proxy
NCM (via Corol. 2). ■\blacksquare

This is obviously not the case for general non-Markovian models, which leads to the very problem of
identification. In these cases, we need to decide whether the mutilation procedure (Corol. 2) can,
in principle, produce the correct answer. We show in Alg. 1 a learning procedure that decides
whether a certain effect is identifiable from observational data. Intuitively, the procedure
searches for two models that respectively minimize and maximize the target query while maintaining
L1L_{1}-consistency with the data distribution. If the L2L_{2} query values induced by the two
models are equal, then the effect is identifiable, and the value is returned; otherwise, the effect
is non-identifiable. Remarkably, the procedure is both necessary and sufficient, which means that
all, and only, identifiable effects are classified as such by our procedure. This implies that,
theoretically, deep learning could be as powerful as the do-calculus in deciding identifiability.
(For a more nuanced discussion of symbolic versus optimization-based approaches for identification,
see Appendix C.4. For non-identifiability examples and further discussion, see C.3.)

4Soundness and Completeness.

Let Ω∗\Omega^{*} be the set of all SCMs, ℳ∗∈Ω∗\mathcal{M}^{*}\in\Omega^{*} be the true SCM inducing
causal diagram 𝒢\mathcal{G}, Q=P⁡(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))Q=P(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x})) be a query of
interest, and Q^\widehat{Q} be the result from running Alg. 1 with inputs P∗​(𝐕)=L1​(ℳ∗)>0P^{*}(\
mathbf{V})=L_{1}(\mathcal{M}^{*})>0, 𝒢\mathcal{G}, and QQ. Then QQ is identifiable from 𝒢\mathcal
{G} and P∗​(𝐕)P^{*}(\mathbf{V}) if and only if Q^\widehat{Q} is not FAIL. Moreover, if Q^\widehat
{Q} is not FAIL, then Q^=Pℳ∗​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))\widehat{Q}=P^{\mathcal{M}^{*}}(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf
{x})). ■\blacksquare

4 The Neural Estimation Problem

While identifiability is fully solved by the asymptotic theory discussed so far (i.e., it is both
necessary and sufficient), we now consider the problem of estimating causal effects in practice
under imperfect optimization and finite samples and computation. For concreteness, we discuss next
the discrete case with binary variables, but our construction extends naturally to categorical and
continuous variables (see Appendix B). We propose next a construction of a 𝒢\mathcal{G}-constrained
NCM M^​(𝒢,𝜽)=⟨𝐔^,𝐕,ℱ^,P⁡(𝐔^)⟩\widehat{M}(\mathcal{G};\bm{\theta})=\langle\widehat{\mathbf{U}},\
mathbf{V},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},P(\widehat{\mathbf{U}})\rangle, which is a possible instantiation
of Def. 7:

 {c})}\left[\prod_{V_{i}\in\mathbf{V}\setminus\mathbf{X}}\tilde{\sigma}_{v_{i}}\right]\approx\
 frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\prod_{V_{i}\in\mathbf{V}\setminus\mathbf{X}}\tilde{\sigma}_{v_{i}},

(\cdot), we work in log-space and use the log-sum-exp trick.

Alg. 1 (lines 2-3) requires non-trivial evaluations of expressions like arg⁡max𝜽​PM^​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))\
arg\max_{\bm{\theta}}P^{\widehat{M}}(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x})) while enforcing L1L_{1}
-consistency. Whenever only finite samples are available {𝐯k}k=1n∼P∗​(𝐕)\{\mathbf{v}_{k}\}_{k=1}^
{n}\sim P^{*}(\mathbf{V}), the parameters of an L1L_{1}-consistent NCM may be estimated by
minimizing data negative log-likelihood:

 𝜽∈\displaystyle arg⁡min𝜽​𝔼P∗​(𝐯)​[−log⁡PM^​(𝒢,𝜽)​(𝐯)]\displaystyle\arg\min_{\bm{\theta}}\

                 theta})}(\mathbf{v})\right]
 ≈\displaystyle\ argmin𝜽1n∑k=1n−logP^mM^​(𝒢,𝜽)(𝐯k).\displaystyle\arg\min_{\bm{\theta}}\frac{1}
 approx          {n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}-\log\widehat{P}_{m}^{\widehat{M}(\mathcal{G};\bm{\theta})}   (4)
                 (\mathbf{v}_{k}).

To simultaneously maximize PM^​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))P^{\widehat{M}}(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x})), we
subtract a weighted second term log⁡P^mM^​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))\log\widehat{P}^{\widehat{M}}_{m}(\mathbf{y}\
mid do(\mathbf{x})), resulting in the objective ℒ⁡({𝐯k}k=1n)\mathcal{L}(\{\mathbf{v}_{k}\}_{k=1}^
{n}) equal to

 1n∑k=1n−logP^mM^(𝐯k)−λlogP^mM^(𝐲∣do(𝐱)),\displaystyle\hskip-7.22743pt\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^
 {n}-\log\widehat{P}_{m}^{\widehat{M}}(\mathbf{v}_{k})-\lambda\log\widehat{P}^{\widehat{M}}_    (5)
 {m}(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x})),

where λ\lambda is initially set to a high value and decreases during training. To minimize, we
instead subtract λ​log⁡(1−P^mM^​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱)))\lambda\log(1-\widehat{P}^{\widehat{M}}_{m}(\mathbf{y}\
mid do(\mathbf{x}))) from the log-likelihood.

Alg. 2 is one possible way of optimizing the parameters 𝜽\bm{\theta} required in lines 2,3 of Alg.
1. Eq. 5 is amenable to optimization through standard gradient descent tools, e.g., (Kingma and
Ba,, 2015; Loshchilov and Hutter,, 2019; Loshchilov and Hutter,, 2017). ^9^9 9 Our approach is
flexible and may take advantage of these different methods depending on the context. There are a
number of alternatives for minimizing the discrepancy between P∗P^{*} and PM^P^{\widehat{M}},
including minimizing divergences, such as maximum mean discrepancy (Gretton et al.,, 2007) or
kernelized Stein discrepancy (Liu et al.,, 2016), performing variational inference (Blei et al.,,
2017), or generative adversarial optimization (Goodfellow et al.,, 2014). ^10^10 10 The NCM can be
extended to the continuous case by replacing the Gumbel-max trick on σ​(ϕi​(⋅))\sigma(\phi_{i}(\
cdot)) with a model that directly computes a probability density given a data point, e.g.,
normalizing flow (Rezende and Mohamed,, 2015) or VAE (Kingma and Welling,, 2014).

One way of understanding Alg. 1 is as a search within the Ω⁡(𝒢)\Omega(\mathcal{G}) space for two 
NCM parameterizations, 𝜽min∗\bm{\theta}^{*}_{\min} and 𝜽max∗\bm{\theta}^{*}_{\max}, that minimizes/
maximizes the interventional distribution, respectively. Whenever the optimization ends, we can
compare the corresponding P⁡(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))P(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x})) and determine whether an
effect is identifiable. With perfect optimization and unbounded resources, identifiability entails
the equality between these two quantities. In practice, we rely on a hypothesis testing step such
as

 |f⁡(M^​(𝜽max))−f⁡(M^​(𝜽min))|<τ|f(\widehat{M}(\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{max}}))-f(\widehat{M}(\bm   (6)
 {\theta}_{\mathrm{min}}))|<\tau

for quantity of interest ff and a certain threshold τ\tau. This threshold is somewhat similar to a
significance level in statistics and can be used to control certain types of errors. In our case,
the threshold τ\tau can be determined empirically. For further discussion, see Appendix B.

5 Experiments

We start by evaluating NCMs (following Eq. 2) in their ability to decide whether an effect is
identifiable through Alg. 2. Observational data is generated from 8 different SCMs, and their
corresponding causal diagrams are shown in Fig. 4 (top part), and Appendix B provides further
details of the parametrizations. Since the NCM does not have access to the true SCM, the causal
diagram and generated datasets are passed to the algorithm to decide whether an effect is
identifiable. The target effect is P⁡(Y∣d​o​(X))P(Y\mid do(X)), and the quantity we optimize is the
average treatment effect (ATE) of XX on YY, A​T​Eℳ​(X,Y)=𝔼ℳ​[Y∣d​o​(X=1)]−𝔼ℳ​[Y∣d​o​(X=0)]{ATE}_{\
mathcal{M}}(X,Y)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{M}}[Y\mid do(X=1)]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{M}}[Y\mid do(X=0)].
Note that if the outcome YY is binary, as in our examples, 𝔼⁡[Y∣d​o​(X=x)]=P⁡(Y=1|d​o​(X=x))\mathbb
{E}[Y\mid do(X=x)]=P(Y=1|do(X=x)). The effect is identifiable through do-calculus in the settings
represented by Fig. 4 in the left part, and not identifiable in right.

Refer to caption
Figure 4: Experimental results on deciding identifiability with NCMs. Top: Graphs from left to
right: (ID cases) back-door, front-door, M, napkin; (not ID cases) bow, extended bow, IV, bad M. 
Middle: Classification accuracy over 3,000 training epochs from running hypothesis test on Eq. 6
with τ=0.01\tau=0.01 (blue), 0.030.03 (green), 0.050.05 (red). Bottom: (1, 5, 10, 25, 50, 75, 90,
95, 99)-percentiles for max-min gaps over 3000 training epochs.

The bottom row of Fig. 4 shows the max-min gaps, the l.h.s of Eq. 6 with f⁡(ℳ)=ATEℳ​(X,Y)f(\mathcal
{M})=\textsc{ATE}_{\mathcal{M}}(X,Y), over 3000 training epochs. The parameter λ\lambda is set to
11 at the beginning, and decreases logarithmically over each epoch until it reaches 0.0010.001 at
the end of training. The max-min gaps can be used to classify the quantity as “ID” or “non-ID”
using the hypothesis testing procedure described in Appendix B. The classification accuracies per
training epoch are shown in Fig. 4 (middle row). Note that in identifiable settings, the gaps
slowly reduce to 0, while the gaps rapidly grow and stay high throughout training in the
unidentifiable ones. The classification accuracy for ID cases then gradually increases as training
progresses, while accuracy for non-ID cases remain high the entire time (perfect in the bow and IV
cases).

Refer to caption
Figure 5: NCM estimation results for ID cases. Columns a, b, c, d correspond to the same graphs as
a, b, c, d in Fig. 4. Top: KL divergence of P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}) induced by naïve model (blue) and
NCM (orange) compared to Pℳ∗​(𝐕)P^{\mathcal{M}^{*}}(\mathbf{V}). Bottom: MAE of ATE of naïve model
(blue), NCM (orange), and WERM (green). Plots in log-log scale.

In the identifiable settings, we also evaluate the performance of the NCM at estimating the correct
causal effect, as shown in Fig. 5. As a generative model, the NCM is capable of generating samples
from both P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}) and identifiable L2L_{2} distributions like P⁡(Y∣d​o​(X))P(Y\mid do
(X)). We compare the NCM to a naïve generative model trained via likelihood maximization fitted on
P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}) without using the inductive bias of the NCM. Since the naïve model is not
defined to sample from P⁡(y∣d​o​(x))P(y\mid do(x)), this shows the implications of arbitrarily
choosing P⁡(y∣d​o​(x))=P⁡(y∣x)P(y\mid do(x))=P(y\mid x). Both models improve at fitting P⁡(𝐕)P(\
mathbf{V}) with more samples, but the naïve model fails to learn the correct ATE except in case
(c), where P⁡(y∣d​o​(x))=P⁡(y∣x)P(y\mid do(x))=P(y\mid x). Further, the NCM is competitive with
WERM Jung et al., 2020b (), a state-of-the-art estimation method that directly targets estimating
the causal effect without generating samples.

6 Conclusions

In this paper, we introduced neural causal models (NCMs) (Def. 3, 18), a special class of SCMs
trainable through gradient-based optimization techniques. We showed that despite being as
expressive as SCMs (Thm. 1), NCMs are unable to perform cross-layer inferences in general (Corol. 1
). Disentangling expressivity and learnability, we formalized a new type of inductive bias based on
non-parametric, structural properties of the generating SCM, accompanied with a constructive
procedure that allows NCMs to represent constraints over the space of interventional distributions
akin to causal diagrams (Thm. 2). We showed that NCMs with this bias retain their full expressivity
(Thm. 3) but are now empowered to solve canonical tasks in causal inference, including the problems
of identification and estimation (Thm. 4). We grounded these results by providing a training
procedure that is both sound and complete (Alg. 1, 2, Cor. 4). Practically speaking, different
neural implementations – combination of architectures, training algorithms, loss functions – can
leverage the framework results introduced in this work (Appendix D.1). We implemented one of such
alternatives as a proof of concept, and experimental results support the feasibility of the
proposed approach. After all, we hope the causal-neural framework established in this paper can
help develop more principled and robust architectures to empower the next generation of AI systems.
We expect these systems to combine the best of both worlds by (1) leveraging causal inference
capabilities of processing the structural invariances found in nature to construct more explainable
and generalizable decision-making procedures, and (2) leveraging deep learning capabilities to
scale inferences to handle challenging, high dimensional settings found in practice.

Appendix A Proofs

In this section, we provide proofs of the statements in the main body of the paper.

A.1 Proofs of Theorem 1 and Corollary 1

In addition to Def. 2, defining layers 1 and 2 of the PCH, we also require a definition for layer
3. While Def. 2 shows how the SCM valuates observational and interventional distributions, the
following definition of layer 3 ((Bareinboim et al.,, 2020, Def. 7)) shows how the SCM valuates
counterfactual distributions, a family of distributions even more expressive than those from lower
layers.

9Layer 3 Valuation.

An SCM ℳ=⟨𝐔,𝐕,ℱ,P⁡(𝐔)⟩\mathcal{M}=\langle\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V},\mathcal{F},P(\mathbf{U})\rangle
induces a family of joint distributions over counterfactual events 𝐘𝐱,…,𝐙𝐰\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{x}},\
ldots,\mathbf{Z}_{\mathbf{w}}, for any 𝐘,𝐙,…,𝐗,𝐖⊆𝐕\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z},\dots,\mathbf{X},\mathbf{W}
\subseteq\mathbf{V}:

 Pℳ​(𝐲𝐱,…,𝐳𝐰)=∑{𝐮∣𝐘𝐱(𝐮)=𝐲,…,𝐙𝐰(𝐮)=𝐳}P⁡(𝐮).\displaystyle P^{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{y}_{\mathbf
 {x}},\dots,\mathbf{z}_{\mathbf{w}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{\mathbf{u}\;\mid\;\mathbf{Y}_   (7)
 {\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{u})=\mathbf{y},\\ \;\;\;\dots,\;\mathbf{Z}_{\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{u})=\
 mathbf{z}\}\end{subarray}}P(\mathbf{u}).

■\blacksquare

For the expressiveness proofs of this paper, we leverage some of the notation and results from (
Zhang and Bareinboim,, 2021). These results focus on the idea of a canonical form of SCMs, first
explored in a special case by (Balke and Pearl,, 1994). Let ℳ=⟨𝐔,𝐕,ℱ,P⁡(𝐔)⟩\mathcal{M}=\langle\
mathbf{U},\mathbf{V},\mathcal{F},P(\mathbf{U})\rangle be any SCM. For each V∈𝐕V\in\mathbf{V}, we
denote ℋV={hV:𝒟𝐩𝐚V→𝒟V}\mathcal{H}_{V}=\{h_{V}:\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{pa}_{V}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_
{V}\} as the set of all possible functions mapping from the domain of the parents 𝐩𝐚V\mathbf{pa}_
{V} to the domain of VV. We will order the elements of ℋV\mathcal{H}_{V} as hV(1),…,hV(mV)h_{V}^
{(1)},\dots,h_{V}^{(m_{V})}, where mV=|ℋV|m_{V}=|\mathcal{H}_{V}|. Since ℋV\mathcal{H}_{V} fully
exhausts all possible functions, we can partition 𝒟𝐔V\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{V}} into sets 𝒟𝐔V
(1),…,𝒟𝐔V(mV)\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{V}}^{(1)},\dots,\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{V}}^{(m_{V})} such
that 𝐮V∈𝒟𝐔V(rV)\mathbf{u}_{V}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{V}}^{(r_{V})} if and only if fV​(⋅,𝐮V)=hV
(rV)f_{V}(\cdot,\mathbf{u}_{V})=h_{V}^{(r_{V})}.

1(Zhang and Bareinboim,, 2021, Lem. 1).

For an SCM ℳ=⟨𝐔,𝐕,ℱ,P⁡(𝐔)⟩\mathcal{M}=\langle\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V},\mathcal{F},P(\mathbf{U})\
rangle, for each V∈𝐕V\in\mathbf{V}, function fV∈ℱf_{V}\in\mathcal{F} can be expressed as

10Canonical SCM.

A canonical SCM is an SCM ℳ=⟨𝐔,𝐕,ℱ,P⁡(𝐔)⟩\mathcal{M}=\langle\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V},\mathcal{F},P(\
mathbf{U})\rangle such that

 1. 1.
   
    𝐔={RV:V∈𝐕}\mathbf{U}=\{R_{V}:V\in\mathbf{V}\}, where 𝒟RV={1,…,mV}\mathcal{D}_{R_{V}}=\{1,\
    dots,m_{V}\} (where mV=|{hV:𝒟𝐩𝐚V→𝒟V}|m_{V}=|\{h_{V}:\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{pa}_{V}}\rightarrow\
    mathcal{D}_{V}\}|) for each V∈𝐕V\in\mathbf{V}.

 2. 2.
   
    For each V∈𝐕V\in\mathbf{V}, fV∈ℱf_{V}\in\mathcal{F} is defined as

     fV​(𝐩𝐚V,rV)=hV(rV)​(𝐩𝐚V).f_{V}(\mathbf{pa}_{V},r_{V})=h_{V}^{(r_{V})}(\mathbf{pa}_{V}).

■\blacksquare

For any SCM ℳ=⟨𝐔,𝐕,ℱ,P⁡(𝐔)⟩\mathcal{M}=\langle\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V},\mathcal{F},P(\mathbf{U})\

ctm},\mathbf{V},\mathcal{F}_{\ctm},P(\mathbf{U}_{\ctm})\rangle such that ℳCM\mathcal{M}_{\ctm} is
L3L_{3}-consistent with ℳ\mathcal{M}. ■\blacksquare

∎

Lemma 2 shows that the canonical SCM can be used as a representative of equivalence classes of
SCMs. In the case where 𝒟𝐕\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}} is discrete, the mapping from an SCM to an
equivalent canonical model conveniently also remaps 𝒟𝐔\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}} to a discrete space.
We next show that any canonical SCM can be constructed in the form of an NCM.

Refer to caption
Figure 6: Example diagram of a neural network node from Definition 11. Nodes on the left are
inputs, numbers on the edges represent weights, and the weighted sum of the inputs is passed
through the binary step activation function.

We will focus on feedforward neural networks, specifically multi-layer perceptrons (MLPs) with the
binary step activation function, even though other types of neural networks could be compatible
with the statement proven here (see Appendix D.1).

11Multi-layer Perceptron.

A neural network node is a function defined as

 f^​(𝐱,𝐰,b)=σ⁡(∑i𝐰i​𝐱i+b),\hat{f}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{w},b)=\sigma\left(\sum_{i}\mathbf{w}_{i}\
 mathbf{x}_{i}+b\right),

where 𝐱\mathbf{x} is a vector of real-valued inputs, 𝐰\mathbf{w} and bb are the real-valued learned
weights and bias respectively, and σ\sigma is an activation function. For this work, we will often
denote σ\sigma as the binary step function for our activation function:

 σ⁡(z)={1z≥00z<0.\sigma(z)=\begin{cases}1&z\geq 0\\ 0&z<0.\end{cases}

This is simply one choice of activation function which always outputs a binary result. (Figure 6
provides an illustration of such a node.)

A neural network layer of width kk is comprised of kk neural network nodes with the same input
vector, together outputting a kk-dimensional output:

 f^​(𝐱,𝐖,𝐛)=(f^1​(𝐱,𝐰1,b1),…,f^k​(𝐱,𝐰k,bk)),\hat{f}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{W},\mathbf{b})=\left(\hat
 {f}_{1}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{w}_{1},b_{1}),\dots,\hat{f}_{k}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{w}_{k},b_{k})\
 right),

where 𝐖={𝐰1,…,𝐰k}\mathbf{W}=\{\mathbf{w}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{w}_{k}\} and 𝐛={b1,…,bk}\mathbf{b}=\{b_
{1},\dots,b_{k}\}. An MLP is defined as a function comprised of several neural network layers f^
1,…,f^ℓ\hat{f}_{1},\dots,\hat{f}_{\ell}, with each layer taking the previous layer’s output as its
input:

 f^MLP​(𝐱)=f^ℓ​(…​f^1​(𝐱,𝐖1,𝐛1)​…,𝐖ℓ,𝐛ℓ).\hat{f}_{\mlp}(\mathbf{x})=\hat{f}_{\ell}(\dots\hat{f}_
 {1}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{W}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1})\dots;\mathbf{W}_{\ell},\mathbf{b}_{\ell}).

This means that a neural network is a function that is a composition of the functions of the
individual layers, where the input is the input to the first layer, and the output is the output of
the last layer. ■\blacksquare

We will show next three basic lemmas (3-5) that will be used later on to help understand the
expressiveness of the networks introduced from Def. 11.

3.

For any function f:𝐗→Yf:\mathbf{X}\rightarrow Y mapping a set of binary variables to a binary
variable, there exists an equivalent MLP f^\hat{f} using binary step activation functions. ■\
blacksquare

Proof.

We define the following three neural network components:

  • •
   
    Given binary input xx, with w=−1w=-1 and b=0b=0, neural network function

     f^NOT​(x)=σ⁡(−x)\hat{f}_{\textsc{NOT}}(x)=\sigma(-x)

    outputs the negation of xx.

  • •
   
    Given binary vector input 𝐱\mathbf{x}, with 𝐰=𝟏\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{1} and b=−1b=-1, neural
    network function

     f^OR​(𝐱)=σ⁡(∑ixi−1)\hat{f}_{\textsc{OR}}(\mathbf{x})=\sigma\left(\sum_{i}x_{i}-1\right)

    outputs the bitwise-OR of 𝐱\mathbf{x}.

  • •
   
    Given binary vector input 𝐱\mathbf{x}, with 𝐰=𝟏\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{1} and b=−|𝐱|b=-|\mathbf{x}|,
    neural network function

     f^AND​(𝐱)=σ⁡(∑ixi−|𝐱|)\hat{f}_{\textsc{AND}}(\mathbf{x})=\sigma\left(\sum_{i}x_{i}-|\mathbf
     {x}|\right)

    outputs the bitwise-AND of 𝐱\mathbf{x}.

Since all functions mapping a set of binary variables to a binary variable can be written in
disjunctive normal form (DNF), we can combine these three components to build f^\hat{f}. ∎

4.

For any function f:𝐗→Yf:\mathbf{X}\rightarrow Y mapping set of variables 𝐗\mathbf{X} to variable
YY, all from finite numerical domains, there exists an equivalent MLP f^\hat{f} using binary step
activations. ■\blacksquare

Proof.

For each value 𝐱∈𝒟𝐗\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{X}}, we first aim to assign a unique binary
representation bin⁡(𝐱)\bin(\mathbf{x}), which we can use more flexibly due to Lemma 3. One simple
way to accomplish this is to map the values to a one-hot encoding, a binary vector for which each
element corresponds to a unique value in 𝒟𝐗\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{X}}.

We will use here a neural function with w=1w=1 and b=−zb=-z, so we have

 f^≥z​(x)=σ⁡(x−z)\hat{f}_{\geq z}(x)=\sigma(x-z)

which, on input xx, outputs 11 if x≤zx\leq z or 00 otherwise. We will also borrow the binary
functions from the proof of Lem. 3.

For each Xi∈𝐗X_{i}\in\mathbf{X} and each xi∈𝒟Xix_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{X_{i}}, we construct neural
network function

 f^=xi​(x)=f^AND​(f^≤xi​(x),(∀xi′<xi)​f^NOT​(f^≤xi′​(x)))\hat{f}_{=x_{i}}(x)=\hat{f}_{\textsc
 {AND}}\left(\hat{f}_{\leq x_{i}}(x),(\forall x^{\prime}_{i}<x_{i})\hat{f}_{\textsc{NOT}}\left(\
 hat{f}_{\leq x^{\prime}_{i}}(x)\right)\right)

where xi′∈𝒟Xix^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{X_{i}}, which, on input x∈𝒟Xix\in\mathcal{D}_{X_{i}},
outputs 11 if x=xix=x_{i} or 0 otherwise.

We can then define for each 𝐳∈𝒟𝐗\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{X}}

 f^=𝐳​(𝐱)=f^AND​(∀i​f^=zi​(xi))\hat{f}_{=\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{x})=\hat{f}_{\textsc{AND}}\left(\
 forall i\hat{f}_{=z_{i}}(x_{i})\right)

which, on input 𝐱∈𝒟𝐗\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{X}}, outputs 1 if 𝐱=𝐳\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{z} or
0 otherwise. Here, xix_{i} and ziz_{i} denote the iith element of 𝐱\mathbf{x} and 𝐳\mathbf{z}
respectively.

We can then define an one-hot binary representation of 𝐱\mathbf{x}, bin⁡(𝐱)\bin(\mathbf{x}), to be
a vector of the outputs of f^=𝐳​(𝐱)\hat{f}_{=\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{x}) for all 𝐳∈𝒟𝐗\mathbf{z}\in\
mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{X}}:

 f^ENC​(𝐱)=(∀(𝐳∈𝒟𝐗)​f^=𝐳​(𝐱))\hat{f}_{\textsc{ENC}}(\mathbf{x})=\left(\forall(\mathbf{z}\in\
 mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{X}})\hat{f}_{=\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{x})\right)

This representation is a binary vector of length |𝒟𝐗||\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{X}}| and is unique for
each value of 𝐱∈𝒟𝐗\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{X}} because f^=𝐳​(𝐱)=1\hat{f}_{=\mathbf{z}}(\
mathbf{x})=1 if and only if 𝐱=𝐳\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{z}, so a different bit is 1 for every choice of 𝐱
\mathbf{x}.

We can similarly define a binary representation for each y∈𝒟Yy\in\mathcal{D}_{Y}, bin⁡(y)\bin(y),
as a binary vector of length |𝒟Y||\mathcal{D}_{Y}|, where each bit corresponds to a value in 𝒟Y\
mathcal{D}_{Y}. If yi∈𝒟Yy_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{Y} is the value that corresponds with the iith bit of
bin⁡(y)\bin(y), then bin⁡(y)i=1\bin(y)_{i}=1 if and only if y=yiy=y_{i}. Now we consider the
translation from bin⁡(y)\bin(y) back into yy using neural networks. We can create the neural
network function on input bin⁡(y)\bin(y) with 𝐰=(yi:yi∈𝒟Y)\mathbf{w}=(y_{i}:y_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_
{Y}) and b=0b=0,

 f^DEC​(bin⁡(y))=𝐰⊺​bin⁡(y),\hat{f}_{\textsc{DEC}}(\bin(y))=\mathbf{w}^{\intercal}\bin(y),

omitting the binary step activation function σ\sigma. This function simply computes the dot product
of bin⁡(y)\bin(y) with a vector of all of the possible values of YY, which results in yy since bin⁡
(y)\bin(y) is 0 in every location except for the bit corresponding to yy.

Combining all of these constructed neural network functions, we can construct a final MLP f^\hat{f}
for mapping 𝐗\mathbf{X} to YY:

 1. 1.
   
    On input 𝐱∈𝒟𝐗\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{X}}, convert 𝐱\mathbf{x} to bin⁡(𝐱)\bin(\mathbf
    {x}) using f^ENC​(𝐱)\hat{f}_{\textsc{ENC}}(\mathbf{x}).

 2. 2.
   
    By Lemma 3, find some MLP mapping bin⁡(𝐱)\bin(\mathbf{x}) to bin⁡(y)\bin(y).

 3. 3.
   
    Finally, use f^DEC\hat{f}_{\textsc{DEC}} to convert bin⁡(y)\bin(y) to yy.

The final MLP f^\hat{f} is the composition of all of the neural networks used to realize these
three steps. ∎

Although neural networks as defined in Def. 11 are undefined for non-numerical inputs and outputs,
any kind of categorical data can be considered if first converted into a numerical representation.

The above two lemmas show that MLPs can be used to express any function, but we will need another
result to incorporate the exogenous sources of randomness. Specifically, we show that MLPs can map
Unif⁡(0,1)\unif(0,1) noise to any other distribution of variables.

5Neural Inverse Probability Integral Transform (Discrete).

For any probability mass function P⁡(𝐗)P(\mathbf{X}), there exists an MLP f^\hat{f} which maps
Unif⁡(0,1)\unif(0,1) to P⁡(𝐗)P(\mathbf{X}). ■\blacksquare

Proof.

Let 𝐱1,𝐱2,…\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\dots be the elements of the support of P⁡(𝐗)P(\mathbf
{X}), ordered arbitrarily. We also define some arbitrary 𝐱0\mathbf{x}_{0} such that P⁡(𝐱0)=0P(\
mathbf{x}_{0})=0. For each i∈{0,1,2,…}i\in\{0,1,2,\dots\}, construct neural network function, with
w=1w=1 and b=−∑j=0iP(𝐱j)b=-\sum_{j=0}^{i}P(\mathbf{x}_{j})

 f^𝐱i​(u)=σ⁡(u−∑j=0iP⁡(𝐱j))\hat{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}(u)=\sigma\left(u-\sum_{j=0}^{i}P(\mathbf{x}_
 {j})\right)

which, on input uu, returns 1 if and only if u≥∑j=0iP⁡(𝐱j)u\geq\sum_{j=0}^{i}P(\mathbf{x}_{j}).
Note that f^𝐱0​(u)\hat{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{0}}(u) is always 1. We then construct a neural network
function f^OUT\hat{f}_{\textsc{OUT}} which, on inputs (f^𝐱0,f^𝐱1,f^𝐱2,…)(\hat{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{0}},\
hat{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{1}},\hat{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{2}},\dots), outputs one of 𝐱1,𝐱2,…\mathbf{x}_{1},\
mathbf{x}_{2},\dots. Specifically, it operates as follows:

 1. 1.
   
    For each i∈{1,2,…}i\in\{1,2,\dots\}, if f^𝐱i=0\hat{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}=0 and f^𝐱i−1=1\hat{f}_{\
    mathbf{x}_{i-1}}=1, then output 𝐱i\mathbf{x}_{i}.

 2. 2.
   
    If none hold, output any arbitrary 𝐱i\mathbf{x}_{i} (this will never happen).

By Lemma 4, we can construct such a function since all f^𝐱i\hat{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{i}} are binary.
Then, let g^​(u)=f^OUT​(f^𝐱0​(u),f^𝐱1​(u),f^𝐱2​(u),…)\hat{g}(u)=\hat{f}_{\textsc{OUT}}(\hat{f}_{\
mathbf{x}_{0}}(u),\hat{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{1}}(u),\hat{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{2}}(u),\dots). Observe that for
uu sampled from Unif⁡(0,1)\unif(0,1),

 P​(g^​(u)=𝐱i)\           =P⁡(f^OUT​(f^𝐱0​(u),f^𝐱1,f^𝐱2,…)=𝐱i)\displaystyle=P\left(\hat{f}_{\
 displaystyle P(\hat{g}   textsc{OUT}}\left(\hat{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{0}}(u),\hat{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{1}},\
 (u)=\mathbf{x}_{i})      hat{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{2}},\dots\right)=\mathbf{x}_{i}\right)
                          =P⁡(f^𝐱i​(u)=0∧f^𝐱i−1​(u)=1)\displaystyle=P\left(\hat{f}_{\mathbf{x}_
                          {i}}(u)=0\wedge\hat{f}_{\mathbf{x}_{i-1}}(u)=1\right)
                          =P⁡(u<∑j=0iP⁡(𝐱j)∧u≥∑j=0i−1P⁡(𝐱j))\displaystyle=P\left(u<\sum_{j=0}^{i}P
                          (\mathbf{x}_{j})\wedge u\geq\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}P(\mathbf{x}_{j})\right)
                          =P⁡(∑j=0i−1P⁡(𝐱j)≤u<∑j=0iP⁡(𝐱j))\displaystyle=P\left(\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}P(\
                          mathbf{x}_{j})\leq u<\sum_{j=0}^{i}P(\mathbf{x}_{j})\right)
                          =∑j=0iP⁡(𝐱j)−∑j=0i−1P⁡(𝐱j)\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{i}P(\mathbf{x}_{j})-
                          \sum_{j=0}^{i-1}P(\mathbf{x}_{j})
                          =P⁡(𝐱i)\displaystyle=P(\mathbf{x}_{i})

for each i∈{1,2,…}i\in\{1,2,\dots\}. Therefore, we see that g^\hat{g} successfully maps the Unif⁡
(0,1)\unif(0,1) distribution to P⁡(𝐗)P(\mathbf{X}). ∎

We can now combine these neural network results with the canonical SCM results to complete the
expressiveness proof for NCMs.

See 1

Proof.

Lemma 2 guarantees that there exists a canonical SCM ℳCM=⟨𝐔CM,𝐕,ℱCM,P⁡(𝐔CM)⟩\mathcal{M}_{\ctm}=\
langle\mathbf{U}_{\ctm},\mathbf{V},\mathcal{F}_{\ctm},P(\mathbf{U}_{\ctm})\rangle that is L3L_{3}
-consistent with ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}. Hence, to construct M^\widehat{M}, it suffices to show how to
construct ℳCM\mathcal{M}_{\ctm} using the architecture of an NCM.

Following the structure of Def. 3, we choose 𝐔^={U^𝐕}\widehat{\mathbf{U}}=\{\widehat{U}_{\mathbf
{V}}\}. For each Vi∈𝐕V_{i}\in\mathbf{V}, we construct f^Vi∈ℱ^\hat{f}_{V_{i}}\in\widehat{\mathcal
{F}} using the following components:

 1. 1.
   
    By Lemma 5, construct f^ViR:𝒟U^𝐕→𝒟𝐔CM\hat{f}_{V_{i}}^{R}:\mathcal{D}_{\widehat{U}_{\mathbf{V}}}
    \rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{\ctm}} such that

     f^ViR​(u^𝐕)=𝐮CM,\hat{f}_{V_{i}}^{R}(\widehat{u}_{\mathbf{V}})=\mathbf{u}_{\ctm},  (13)

    where

     PM^​(f^ViR​(U^𝐕)=𝐮CM)=PℳCM​(𝐮CM).P^{\widehat{M}}\left(\hat{f}_{V_{i}}^{R}(\widehat{U}_{\  (14)
     mathbf{V}})=\mathbf{u}_{\ctm}\right)=P^{\mathcal{M}_{\ctm}}(\mathbf{u}_{\ctm}).

 2. 2.
   
    By Lemma 4, construct f^ViH:𝒟𝐏𝐚Vi×𝒟𝐔CM→𝒟Vi\hat{f}_{V_{i}}^{H}:\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{i}}}
    \times\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{U}_{\ctm}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{V_{i}} such that

     f^ViH​(𝐩𝐚Vi,𝐮CM)\displaystyle\hat{f}_{V_{i}}^  =fViCM​(𝐩𝐚Vi,rVi)\displaystyle=f_{V_{i}}^  (15)
     {H}(\mathbf{pa}_{V_{i}},\mathbf{u}_{\ctm})     {\ctm}(\mathbf{pa}_{V_{i}},r_{V_{i}})
                                                    =hVi(rV)​(𝐩𝐚Vi)\displaystyle=h_{V_{i}}^    (16)
                                                    {(r_{V})}(\mathbf{pa}_{V_{i}})

    where rVir_{V_{i}} is the value in 𝐮CM\mathbf{u}_{\ctm} corresponding to ViV_{i}.

Combining these two components leads to MLP

 f^Vi​(𝐩𝐚Vi,u^𝐕)=f^ViH​(𝐩𝐚Vi,f^ViR​(u^𝐕))\hat{f}_{V_{i}}(\mathbf{pa}_{V_{i}},\widehat{u}_{\
 mathbf{V}})=\hat{f}_{V_{i}}^{H}\left(\mathbf{pa}_{V_{i}},\hat{f}_{V_{i}}^{R}(\widehat{u}_{\   (17)
 mathbf{V}})\right)

Although this does not exactly fit the structure in Def. 11 because 𝐩𝐚V\mathbf{pa}_{V} is not
included as an input into f^ViR\hat{f}_{V_{i}}^{R}, this can be altered by simply having f^ViR\hat
{f}_{V_{i}}^{R} accepting 𝐩𝐚V\mathbf{pa}_{V} as an input and outputting it alongside 𝐮CM\mathbf{u}_
{\ctm} without changing it.

By Eqs. 14 and 16, the NCM M^\widehat{M} is constructed to match ℳCM\mathcal{M}_{\ctm} on all
outputs. Hence, for any counterfactual query 𝛗\bm{\upvarphi}, we have

 ℳCM⊧𝛗⇔M^⊧𝛗\mathcal{M}_{\ctm}\models\bm{\upvarphi}\Leftrightarrow\widehat{M}\models\bm{\upvarphi}

and therefore

 ℳ∗⊧𝛗⇔M^⊧𝛗.\mathcal{M}^{*}\models\bm{\upvarphi}\Leftrightarrow\widehat{M}\models\bm{\upvarphi}.

∎

While Thm. 1 demonstrates the expressive power of an SCM parameterized by neural networks, we now
consider its limitations. Notably, we show in the sequel that NCMs suffer from the same
consequences implied by the CHT.

1Causal Hierarchy Theorem (CHT) (Bareinboim et al.,, 2020, Thm. 1).

Let Ω∗\Omega^{*} be the set of all SCMs. We say that Layer jj of the causal hierarchy for SCMs
collapses to Layer ii (i<ji<j) relative to ℳ∗∈Ω∗\mathcal{M}^{*}\in\Omega^{*} if Li​(ℳ∗)=Li​(ℳ)L_{i}
(\mathcal{M}^{*})=L_{i}(\mathcal{M}) implies that Lj​(ℳ∗)=Lj​(ℳ)L_{j}(\mathcal{M}^{*})=L_{j}(\
mathcal{M}) for all ℳ∈Ω∗\mathcal{M}\in\Omega^{*}. Then, with respect to the Lebesgue measure over
(a suitable encoding of L3L_{3}-equivalence classes of) SCMs, the subset in which Layer jj of NCMs
collapses to Layer ii is measure zero. ■\blacksquare

We prove a similar result for NCMs as a corollary of Fact 1 and Thm. 1.

See 1

Proof.

Since all NCMs are SCMs, an SCM-collapse with respect to ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} also implies an NCM
-collapse with respect to ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}.

If layer jj does not SCM-collapse to layer ii with respect to ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}, then there exists
an SCM ℳ\mathcal{M} such that Li​(ℳ∗)=Li​(ℳ)L_{i}(\mathcal{M}^{*})=L_{i}(\mathcal{M}) but Lj​(ℳ∗)
≠Lj​(ℳ)L_{j}(\mathcal{M}^{*})\neq L_{j}(\mathcal{M}). By Thm. 1, this implies that there exists an 
NCM ℳ^\widehat{\mathcal{M}} such that Li​(ℳ∗)=Li​(ℳ^)L_{i}(\mathcal{M}^{*})=L_{i}(\widehat{\mathcal
{M}}) but Lj​(ℳ∗)≠Lj​(ℳ^)L_{j}(\mathcal{M}^{*})\neq L_{j}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}}), which means that
layer jj also does not NCM-collapse to layer ii.

These two statements imply that the set of SCMs that undergo some form of SCM-collapse is
equivalent to the set of SCMs that undergo some form of NCM-collapse. Therefore, the result from
Fact 1 must also hold for NCMs. ∎

A.2 Proof of Theorem 2

The results proven in this section involve the incorporation of structural constraints, as
introduced through the graphical treatment provided in (Pearl,, 1995), and made it explicit and
generalized for models with latent variables in (Bareinboim et al.,, 2020, Sec.1. 4). For
convenience, we list the basic definitions below, but refer the readers to the references for more
detailed explanations and further examples.
