We refer to 𝒢\mathcal{G} as the causal diagram induced by ℳ\mathcal{M} (or “causal diagram of ℳ\
mathcal{M}” for short). ■\blacksquare

13Confounded Component (Bareinboim et al.,, 2020, Def. 14).

Let GG be a causal diagram. Let {𝐂1,𝐂2,…,𝐂k}\{\mathbf{C}_{1},\mathbf{C}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{C}_{k}\}
be a partition over the set of variables 𝐕\mathbf{V}, where 𝐂i\mathbf{C}_{i} is said to be a
confounded component (C-component for short) of GG if for every Va,Vb∈𝐂iV_{a},V_{b}\in\mathbf{C}_
{i}, there exists a path made entirely of bidirected edges between VaV_{a} and VbV_{b} in GG, and
𝐂i\mathbf{C}_{i} is maximal. We denote 𝐂⁡(Va)\mathbf{C}(V_{a}) as the C-component containing VaV_
{a}. ■\blacksquare

14Semi-Markov Relative (Bareinboim et al.,, 2020, Def. 15).

A distribution PP is said to be semi-Markov relative to a graph GG if for any topological order <<
of GG through its directed edges, PP factorizes as

 P⁡(𝐯)=∏Vi∈𝐕P⁡(vi∣𝐩𝐚Vi+),P(\mathbf{v})=\prod_{V_{i}\in\mathbf{V}}P(v_{i}\mid\mathbf{pa}_{V_    (18)
 {i}}^{+}),

where 𝐏𝐚Vi+=𝐏𝐚⁡({V∈𝐂⁡(Vi):V≤Vi})\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{i}}^{+}=\mathbf{Pa}(\{V\in\mathbf{C}(V_{i}):V\leq
V_{i}\}), with ≤\leq referring to the topological ordering. ■\blacksquare

15Causal Bayesian Network (CBN) (Bareinboim et al.,, 2020, Def. 16).

Given observed variables 𝐕\mathbf{V}, let 𝐏∗\mathbf{P}^{*} be the collection of all interventional
distributions P⁡(𝐕∣d​o​(𝐱))P(\mathbf{V}\mid do(\mathbf{x})), 𝐗⊆𝐕\mathbf{X}\subseteq\mathbf{V}, 𝐱∈𝒟𝐗
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{X}}. A causal diagram 𝒢\mathcal{G} is a Causal Bayesian Network
for 𝐏∗\mathbf{P}^{*} if for every intervention d​o​(𝐗=𝐱)do(\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{x}) and every
topological ordering << of 𝒢X¯\mathcal{G}_{\overline{X}} through its directed edges,

 1. (i)
   
    P⁡(𝐕∣d​o​(𝐗=𝐱))P(\mathbf{V}\mid do(\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{x})) is semi-Markov relative to 𝒢𝐗¯\
    mathcal{G}_{\overline{\mathbf{X}}}.

 2. (ii)
   
    For every Vi∈𝐕∖𝐗V_{i}\in\mathbf{V}\setminus\mathbf{X}, 𝐖⊆𝐕∖(𝐏𝐚i𝐗+∪𝐗∪{Vi})\mathbf{W}\subseteq\
    mathbf{V}\setminus(\mathbf{Pa}_{i}^{\mathbf{X}+}\cup\mathbf{X}\cup\{V_{i}\}):

     P⁡(vi∣d​o​(𝐱),𝐩𝐚i𝐱+,d​o​(𝐰))=P⁡(vi∣d​o​(𝐱),𝐩𝐚i𝐱+)P(v_{i}\mid do(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{pa}_{i}^
     {\mathbf{x}+},do(\mathbf{w}))=P(v_{i}\mid do(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{pa}_{i}^{\mathbf{x}+})

    ,

 3. (iii)
   
    For every Vi∈𝐕∖𝐗V_{i}\in\mathbf{V}\setminus\mathbf{X}, let 𝐏𝐚i𝐗+\mathbf{Pa}_{i}^{\mathbf{X}+}
    be partitioned into two sets of confounded and unconfounded parents, 𝐏𝐚ic\mathbf{Pa}_{i}^{c}
    and 𝐏𝐚iu\mathbf{Pa}_{i}^{u} in 𝒢𝐗¯\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\mathbf{X}}}. Then

      P⁡(vi∣d​o​(𝐱),𝐩𝐚ic,d​o​(𝐩𝐚iu))\displaystyle P(v_{i}\mid do(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{pa}_{i}^
      {c},do(\mathbf{pa}_{i}^{u}))
      =P⁡(vi∣d​o​(𝐱),𝐩𝐚ic,𝐩𝐚iu)\displaystyle=P(v_{i}\mid do(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{pa}_{i}^{c},\
      mathbf{pa}_{i}^{u})

Here, 𝐏𝐚Vi𝐱+=𝐏𝐚⁡({V∈𝐂𝐗¯​(Vi):V≤Vi})\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{i}}^{\mathbf{x}+}=\mathbf{Pa}(\{V\in\mathbf{C}_
{\overline{\mathbf{X}}}(V_{i}):V\leq V_{i}\}), with 𝐂𝐗¯\mathbf{C}_{\overline{\mathbf{X}}} referring
to the corresponding C-component in 𝒢𝐗¯\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\mathbf{X}}} and ≤\leq referring to
the topological ordering. ■\blacksquare

In fact, for any SCM ℳ\mathcal{M}, its induced causal diagram and interventional distributions form
a CBN. This means that the diagram encodes the qualitative constraints induced over the space of
interventional distributions, despite the specific values that these distributions attain and the ℱ
\mathcal{F} and P⁡(𝐔)P(\mathbf{U}) of ℳ\mathcal{M}.

2SCM-CBN L2L_{2} connection (Bareinboim et al.,, 2020, Thm. 4).

The causal diagram 𝒢\mathcal{G} induced by SCM ℳ\mathcal{M} is a CBN for L2​(ℳ)L_{2}(\mathcal{M}).
■\blacksquare

We can now show that, indeed, all of the CBN constraints implied by a causal diagram 𝒢\mathcal{G}
are encoded in a 𝒢\mathcal{G}-constrained NCM constructed via Def. 7.

6.

Let M^​(𝛉)=⟨𝐔^,𝐕,ℱ^,P^​(𝐔^)⟩\widehat{M}(\bm{\theta})=\langle\widehat{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{V},\
widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{P}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}})\rangle be a 𝒢\mathcal{G}-constrained NCM.
Let 𝒢^\widehat{\mathcal{G}} be the causal diagram induced by M^\widehat{M}. Then 𝒢^=𝒢\widehat{\
mathcal{G}}=\mathcal{G}. ■\blacksquare

Proof.

Considering Def. 12 in the context of 𝒢^\widehat{\mathcal{G}}’s construction, note that by step 1
all of the vertices match, simply having one for each variable in 𝐕\mathbf{V}.

Step 2 adds a directed edge from ViV_{i} to VjV_{j} if f^Vi\hat{f}_{V_{i}} has VjV_{j} as an
argument. By step 2 of Def. 7, 𝐩𝐚Vj\mathbf{pa}_{V_{j}} will contain ViV_{i} if and only if there
was a directed edge from ViV_{i} to VjV_{j} in 𝒢\mathcal{G}. This implies that f^Vj\hat{f}_{V_{j}}
will contain ViV_{i} as an argument if and only if there was a directed edge from ViV_{i} to VjV_
{j} in 𝒢\mathcal{G}, so the directed edges must also match in 𝒢^\widehat{\mathcal{G}}.

Finally, step 3 of Def. 12 states that a bidirected edge between ViV_{i} and VjV_{j} is added to 𝒢^
\widehat{\mathcal{G}} when f^Vi\hat{f}_{V_{i}} and f^Vj\hat{f}_{V_{j}} share some U^∈𝐔^\widehat{U}\
in\widehat{\mathbf{U}} as an argument or have arguments from 𝐔^\widehat{\mathbf{U}} that are not
independent. Def. 3 ensures that all variables in 𝐔^\widehat{\mathbf{U}} are independent, so a
shared U^∈𝐔^\widehat{U}\in\widehat{\mathbf{U}} between functions in ℱ^\widehat{\mathcal{F}} is the
only way a bidirected edge would be generated in 𝒢^\widehat{\mathcal{G}}. Step 1 of Def. 7
constructs U^\widehat{U} such that it contains some U^𝐂\widehat{U}_{\mathbf{C}} if and only if 𝐂\
mathbf{C} is a C2C^{2}-component in 𝒢\mathcal{G}. If Vi,Vj∈𝐕V_{i},V_{j}\in\mathbf{V} are connected
by a bidirected edge in 𝒢\mathcal{G}, then there must exist some C2C^{2}-component 𝐂\mathbf{C} in 𝒢
\mathcal{G} such that Vi,Vj∈𝐂∗V_{i},V_{j}\in\mathbf{C}^{*}, so there must exist U^𝐂∈𝐔^\widehat{U}_
{\mathbf{C}}\in\widehat{\mathbf{U}}. Hence, since U^𝐂\widehat{U}_{\mathbf{C}} is shared by both f^
Vi\hat{f}_{V_{i}} and f^Vj\hat{f}_{V_{j}}, the corresponding bidirected edge in 𝒢\mathcal{G} must
also match in 𝒢^\widehat{\mathcal{G}}.

Therefore, since all vertices and edges match between 𝒢\mathcal{G} and 𝒢^\widehat{\mathcal{G}}, we
can conclude that 𝒢=𝒢^\mathcal{G}=\widehat{\mathcal{G}}. ∎

See 2

Proof.

This follows directly from Lemma 6 and Fact 2. ∎

A.3 Proof of Theorem 3

For this proof, we expand on the technical results provided in Zhang et al., (2021). The paper
works with a subclass of SCMs known as discrete SCMs and proves strong results about its
expressiveness. Similar to this paper, we will assume that in any SCM, the variables in 𝐔\mathbf{U}
are independent, and unobserved confounding is modeled by sharing the same variable from 𝐔\mathbf
{U} in the functions of multiple variables in 𝐕\mathbf{V}.

16Discrete SCM (Zhang et al.,, 2021, Def. 2).

An SCM ℳ=⟨𝐔,𝐕,ℱ,P⁡(𝐔)⟩\mathcal{M}=\langle\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V},\mathcal{F},P(\mathbf{U})\rangle is
said to be a discrete SCM if 𝒟U\mathcal{D}_{U} is discrete for all U∈𝐔U\in\mathbf{U} and 𝒟V\mathcal
{D}_{V} is both discrete and finite for all V∈𝐕V\in\mathbf{V}. ■\blacksquare

3(Zhang et al.,, 2021, Thm. 1).

Let Ω∗\Omega^{*} be the set of all SCMs and Ω′\Omega^{\prime} the set of discrete SCMs. For any SCM
ℳ∗∈Ω∗\mathcal{M}^{*}\in\Omega^{*} inducing causal diagram 𝒢\mathcal{G}, there exists a discrete SCM
ℳ′∈Ω′\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\in\Omega^{\prime} with finite |𝒟U||\mathcal{D}_{U}| for all U∈𝐔U\in\
mathbf{U} such that ℳ′\mathcal{M}^{\prime} is 𝒢\mathcal{G}-consistent and is L3L_{3}-consistent
with ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}. ■\blacksquare

We can now combine these results to achieve an expressiveness result on the counterfactual level
for NCMs.

See 3

Proof.

By Fact 3, there must exist a discrete SCM ℳ′=⟨𝐔′,𝐕,ℱ′,P⁡(𝐔′)⟩\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\langle\mathbf
{U}^{\prime},\mathbf{V},\mathcal{F}^{\prime},P(\mathbf{U}^{\prime})\rangle such that ℳ′\mathcal{M}^
{\prime} is 𝒢\mathcal{G}-consistent, is L3L_{3}-consistent with ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} (implying L2L_{2}
-consistency), and |𝒟U||\mathcal{D}_{U}| is finite for all U∈𝐔U\in\mathbf{U}. Let M^=⟨𝐔^,𝐕,ℱ^,P⁡(𝐔
^)⟩\widehat{M}=\langle\widehat{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{V},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},P(\widehat{\mathbf{U}})
\rangle be a 𝒢\mathcal{G}-NCM such that each f^V∈ℱ^\hat{f}_{V}\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}} is an MLP
composed of smaller MLPs as defined next.

For U∈𝐔′U\in\mathbf{U}^{\prime}, let 𝐕U⊆𝐕\mathbf{V}_{U}\subseteq\mathbf{V} denote the set of
endogenous variables such that for all V∈𝐕UV\in\mathbf{V}_{U}, f^V\hat{f}_{V} takes UU as an
argument. Let ℂ={𝐂1,…,𝐂k}\mathbb{C}=\{\mathbf{C}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{C}_{k}\}, with 𝐂1,…​𝐂k⊆𝐕\mathbf
{C}_{1},\dots\mathbf{C}_{k}\subseteq\mathbf{V} denote the set of C2C^{2}-components of 𝒢\mathcal
{G}. Let 𝐔𝐂1′,…,𝐔𝐂k′\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{C}_{1}},\dots,\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{C}_{k}}
be a partition over 𝐔′\mathbf{U}^{\prime} such that if U∈𝐔𝐂i′U\in\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{C}_
{i}}, then 𝐕U⊆𝐂i\mathbf{V}_{U}\subseteq\mathbf{C}_{i}. If for any U∈𝐔′U\in\mathbf{U}^{\prime},
there exist multiple components such that this applies, one can be chosen arbitrarily. For each VV,
let ℂ⁡(V)⊆ℂ\mathbb{C}(V)\subseteq\mathbb{C} denote the set of components that contain VV. Then we
note that

 𝐔V′⊆⋃𝐂∈ℂ⁡(V)𝐔𝐂′,\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{V}\subseteq\bigcup_{\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{C}(V)}\mathbf{U}^{\
 prime}_{\mathbf{C}},

where 𝐔V′⊆𝐔′\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{V}\subseteq\mathbf{U}^{\prime} denotes the exogenous parents of VV
in ℳ′\mathcal{M}^{\prime}.

By construction of the 𝒢\mathcal{G}-NCM in Def. 7, 𝐔^\widehat{\mathbf{U}} consists of a Unif⁡(0,1)\
unif(0,1) random variable U^𝐂i\widehat{U}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}} for each C2C^{2}-component 𝐂i\mathbf{C}_
{i}. By Lemma 5, we can construct MLP f^𝐂i(𝐔′)\hat{f}^{(\mathbf{U}^{\prime})}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
mapping U^𝐂i\widehat{U}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}} to 𝐔𝐂i′\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}} for each 𝐂i\
mathbf{C}_{i}. Then by Lemma 4, we can construct MLP f^V(ℱ′)\hat{f}_{V}^{(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})} to
map 𝐔V′\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{V} and 𝐏𝐚V\mathbf{Pa}_{V} to VV, matching fV′f^{\prime}_{V}. Combining
these two results, f^V​(𝐩𝐚V,𝐮^V)\hat{f}_{V}(\mathbf{pa}_{V},\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{V}) is defined as
f^V(ℱ′)​(𝐩𝐚V,f^𝐂i1(𝐔′)​(u^𝐂i1),…,f^𝐂iℓ(𝐔′)​(u^𝐂iℓ))\hat{f}_{V}^{(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})}(\mathbf{pa}
_{V},\hat{f}^{(\mathbf{U}^{\prime})}_{{\mathbf{C}_{i_{1}}}}(\widehat{u}_{\mathbf{C}_{i_{1}}}),\
dots,\hat{f}^{(\mathbf{U}^{\prime})}_{\mathbf{C}_{i_{\ell}}}(\widehat{u}_{\mathbf{C}_{i_{\ell}}})),
where ℂ⁡(V)={𝐂i1,…,𝐂iℓ}\mathbb{C}(V)=\{\mathbf{C}_{i_{1}},\dots,\mathbf{C}_{i_{\ell}}\}. ^11^11 11
There are a few subtleties here to align with Def. 11. First, although ⋃𝐂∈ℂ⁡(V)𝐔𝐂′\bigcup_{\mathbf
{C}\in\mathbb{C}(V)}\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{C}} may contain elements of 𝐔′\mathbf{U}^{\prime}
not in 𝐔V′\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{V}, f^V(ℱ′)\hat{f}_{V}^{(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})} can be constructed
to accept them as input and not use them (weight of 0, identity activation function). Secondly,
although Def. 11 does not allow additional inputs inbetween layers, 𝐩𝐚V\mathbf{pa}_{V} can simply
be provided as an input to f^𝐂(𝐔′)\hat{f}^{(\mathbf{U}^{\prime})}_{\mathbf{C}} and passed forward
to the next layer without modification (weight of 1, identity activation function). Thirdly, while
Def. 11 is not defined to have multiple nested MLPs in the same layer, the same result can be
achieved by nesting them iteratively and passing the relevant outputs through the nested layers
without modification. The presentation in the proof is made for the sake of clarity.

Altogether, ℱ^(𝐔′)={f^𝐂(𝐔′):𝐂∈ℂ}\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{(\mathbf{U}^{\prime})}=\{\hat{f}^{(\mathbf

neural mapping from 𝐔′\mathbf{U}^{\prime} to 𝐕\mathbf{V}. We use the notation ℱ𝐱​(𝐔)⊧𝐲\mathcal{F}_
{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{U})\models\mathbf{y} to be equivalent to the statement 𝐘𝐱​(𝐔)=𝐲\mathbf{Y}_{\

variables of 𝐘\mathbf{Y} under intervention 𝐗=𝐱\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{x} takes value 𝐲\mathbf{y}.

Then, for any interventional query Q=P⁡(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))=P⁡(𝐘𝐱=𝐲)Q=P(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x}))=P\
left(\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{y}\right), we have

 PM^​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))\displaystyle  =P​(𝐘^𝐱​(𝐔^)=𝐲)\displaystyle=P\left(\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}_{\mathbf

A.4 Proofs of Theorem 4 and Corollaries 2 and 4

See 4

Proof.

Since Ω⊂Ω∗\Omega\subset\Omega^{*}, it must be the case that identifiability in 𝒢\mathcal{G} and P⁡
(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}) implies identifiability in Ω⁡(𝒢)\Omega(\mathcal{G}) and P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}).
Identifiability in 𝒢\mathcal{G} and P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}) implies that all pairs of SCMs that match in
𝒢\mathcal{G} and P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}) will also match in QQ. This means that for any SCM ℳ∗\mathcal
{M}^{*} that induces P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}) and 𝒢\mathcal{G}, we must have Pℳ1​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))=Pℳ2​(𝐲∣d​o​
(𝐱))P^{\mathcal{M}_{1}}(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x}))=P^{\mathcal{M}_{2}}(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\
mathbf{x})) for all pairs of SCMs ℳ1,ℳ2∈Ω∗\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2}\in\Omega^{*} that induce
𝒢\mathcal{G} such that L1​(ℳ1)=L1​(ℳ2)=P⁡(𝐕)L_{1}(\mathcal{M}_{1})=L_{1}(\mathcal{M}_{2})=P(\mathbf
{V}). Given that all NCMs are SCMs, and 𝒢\mathcal{G}-constrained NCMs are 𝒢\mathcal{G}-consistent
by Thm. 2, the set of all 𝒢\mathcal{G}-consistent SCMs includes the set of all 𝒢\mathcal{G}
-constrained NCMs. Hence, this match in QQ should also hold if ℳ1\mathcal{M}_{1} and ℳ2\mathcal{M}_
{2} are NCMs.

If QQ is not identifiable from 𝒢\mathcal{G} and P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}), there must exist ℳ1,ℳ2∈Ω∗\
mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2}\in\Omega^{*} such that ℳ1\mathcal{M}_{1} and ℳ2\mathcal{M}_{2} both
induce 𝒢\mathcal{G}, L1​(ℳ1)=L1​(ℳ2)=P⁡(𝐕)>0L_{1}(\mathcal{M}_{1})=L_{1}(\mathcal{M}_{2})=P(\mathbf
{V})>0, but Pℳ1​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))≠Pℳ2​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))P^{\mathcal{M}_{1}}(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x}))\neq P
^{\mathcal{M}_{2}}(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x})). Theorem 3 states that there must exist NCMs M^
1,M^2∈Ω⁡(𝒢)\widehat{M}_{1},\widehat{M}_{2}\in\Omega(\mathcal{G}) such that L2​(ℳ1^)=L2​(ℳ1)L_{2}(\
widehat{\mathcal{M}_{1}})=L_{2}(\mathcal{M}_{1}) and L2​(ℳ2^)=L2​(ℳ2)L_{2}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}_
{2}})=L_{2}(\mathcal{M}_{2}). This implies that, for any SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} inducing 𝒢\mathcal
{G} and observational distribution P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}), there exist these two 𝒢\mathcal{G}
-constrained NCMs that are L1L_{1}-consistent with ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} (since they are L2L_{2}
-consistent with ℳ1\mathcal{M}_{1} and ℳ2\mathcal{M}_{2} respectively), yet they do not match in QQ
(i.e. PM^1​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))≠PM^2​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))P^{\widehat{M}_{1}}(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x}))\neq P^{\
widehat{M}_{2}}(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x}))). In other words, if QQ is not identifiable from 𝒢\
mathcal{G} and P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}), then it is also not identifiable from Ω⁡(𝒢)\Omega(\mathcal{G})
and P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}). ∎

See 2

Proof.

By Thm. 3, there must exist a 𝒢\mathcal{G}-constrained NCM M^′∈Ω⁡(𝒢)\widehat{M}^{\prime}\in\Omega(\

(𝐕)L_{1}(\widehat{M})=P(\mathbf{V}). This means that PM^​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))=Pℳ∗​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))P^{\widehat{M}}
(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x}))=P^{\mathcal{M}^{*}}(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x})) for arbitrary 𝒢\
mathcal{G}-constrained NCMs M^\widehat{M} that match P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}). Finally, PM^​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))P^
{\widehat{M}}(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x})) can be computed from M^\widehat{M} using Def. 2. ∎

See 4

Proof.

Theorem 4 states that QQ must be identifiable from 𝒢\mathcal{G} and P∗​(𝐕)P^{*}(\mathbf{V}) if and
only if for all pairs of 𝒢\mathcal{G}-consistent NCMs, M^1,M^2∈Ω⁡(𝒢)\widehat{M}_{1},\widehat{M}_{2}
\in\Omega(\mathcal{G}) with L1​(M^1)=L1​(M^2)=P∗​(𝐕)>0L_{1}(\widehat{M}_{1})=L_{1}(\widehat{M}_{2})
=P^{*}(\mathbf{V})>0, PM^1​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))=PM^2​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))P^{\widehat{M}_{1}}(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\
mathbf{x}))=P^{\widehat{M}_{2}}(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x})). This holds if and only if PM^​
(𝜽min∗)​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))=PM^​(𝜽max∗)​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))P^{\widehat{M}(\bm{\theta}^{*}_{\min})}(\mathbf{y}\mid
do(\mathbf{x}))=P^{\widehat{M}(\bm{\theta}^{*}_{\max})}(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x})). If they are
not equal, then M^​(𝜽min∗)\widehat{M}(\bm{\theta}^{*}_{\min}) and M^​(𝜽max∗)\widehat{M}(\bm{\theta}
^{*}_{\max}) are a counterexample of two NCMs that do not match for QQ. Otherwise, if they are
equal, then all other NCMs must also induce the same answer for QQ. A result for QQ that is less
than PM^​(𝜽min∗)​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))P^{\widehat{M}(\bm{\theta}^{*}_{\min})}(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x}))
or greater than PM^​(𝜽max∗)​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))P^{\widehat{M}(\bm{\theta}^{*}_{\max})}(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\
mathbf{x})) would contradict the optimality of 𝜽min∗\bm{\theta}^{*}_{\min} and 𝜽max∗\bm{\theta}^{*}
_{\max}.

If QQ is identifiable, then Corollary 2 guarantees that any NCM that induces P∗​(𝐕)P^{*}(\mathbf
{V}) and 𝒢\mathcal{G} will induce the correct Pℳ∗​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))P^{\mathcal{M}^{*}}(\mathbf{y}\mid do
(\mathbf{x})). ∎

A.5 Proof of Corollary 3

In our discussion of the identification problem in Sec. 3, we stated Corol. 3 as the solution to a
special class of models known as Markovian. In SCMs, Markovianity implies that all variables in 𝐔\
mathbf{U} are independent and not shared between functions. Correspondingly, this means that no
variable in 𝐔^\widehat{\mathbf{U}} of an NCM can be associated with more than one function. In the
causal diagram, this implies that there are no bidirected edges.

We emphasize that Markovianity is a strong constraint in many settings, and the following corollary
from (Bareinboim et al.,, 2020) illustrates that identification in the Markovian setting is quite
trivial.

4(Bareinboim et al.,, 2020, Corol. 2).

In Markovian models (i.e., models without unobserved confounding), for any treatment 𝐗\mathbf{X}
and outcome 𝐘\mathbf{Y}, the interventional distribution P⁡(𝐘∣d​o​(𝐱))P(\mathbf{Y}\mid do(\mathbf
{x})) is always identifiable and given by the expression

 P⁡(𝐘∣d​o​(𝐱))=∑𝐳P⁡(𝐘∣𝐱,𝐳)​P​(𝐳),P(\mathbf{Y}\mid do(\mathbf{x}))=\sum_{\mathbf{z}}P(\mathbf   (19)
 {Y}\mid\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})P(\mathbf{z}),

where 𝐙\mathbf{Z} is the set of all variables not affected by the action 𝐗\mathbf{X}
(non-descendants of 𝐗\mathbf{X}). ■\blacksquare

In other words, every query in a Markovian setting can be identified via Eq. 19, also known as the
backdoor adjustment formula. Naturally, this result extends to identifiability via mutilation in 
NCMs due to the connection between neural identification and graph identification.

See 3

Proof.

Fact 4 shows that any query P⁡(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))P(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x})) is identifiable from 𝒢\
mathcal{G} and P⁡(𝐯)P(\mathbf{v}) in Markovian settings. Hence, by Thm. 4, it must also be
identifiable from Ω⁡(𝒢)\Omega(\mathcal{G}) and P⁡(𝐯)P(\mathbf{v}). Moreover, Corol. 2 states that
the effect can be computed via the mutilation procedure on a proxy 𝒢\mathcal{G}-constrained NCM M^\
widehat{M} with L1​(M^)=P​(𝐯)L_{1}(\widehat{M})=P(\mathbf{v}). ∎

Without the Markovianity assumption, the identification problem becomes significantly more
challenging. As we show in Example 2 from Appendix C.1, a query can be non-ID even in a two
variable case whenever unobserved confounding cannot be ruled out.

Appendix B Experimental Details

This section provides the detailed information about our experiments. Code for the experiments can
be found at https://github.com/CausalAILab/NeuralCausalModels. Our models are primarily written in
PyTorch (Paszke et al.,, 2017), and training is facilitated using PyTorch Lightning (Falcon and
Cho,, 2020).

B.1 Data Generation Process

The NCM architecture follows the description in Eq. 2. For each function, we use a MADE module (
Germain et al.,, 2015) following the implementation in (Karpathy,, 2018) (MIT license). Each MADE
module uses 4 hidden layers of size 32 with ReLU activations. For each complete confounded
component’s unobserved confounder, we use uniform noise with dimension equal to the sum of the
complete confounded component’s variables’ dimensions.

To generate data for our experiments, we use a version of the canonical SCM similar to Def. 10.
Given data from P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}) and a graph 𝒢\mathcal{G}, we construct a canonical SCM ℳCM=
⟨𝐔CM,𝐕,ℱCM,P⁡(𝐔CM)⟩\mathcal{M}_{\ctm}=\langle\mathbf{U}_{\ctm},\mathbf{V},\mathcal{F}_{\ctm},P(\
mathbf{U}_{\ctm})\rangle such that for each V∈𝐕V\in\mathbf{V}, 𝐩𝐚V\mathbf{pa}_{V} contains the set
of variables with a directed edge into VV in 𝒢\mathcal{G}. For any bidirected edge between V1V_{1}
and V2V_{2}, we introduce a dependency between the noise generated from RV1R_{V_{1}} and RV1R_{V_
{1}}. Otherwise, variables in 𝐔CM\mathbf{U}_{\ctm} are independent.

The canonical SCM is implemented in PyTorch, with trainable parameters that determine P⁡(𝐔CM)P(\
mathbf{U}_{\ctm}). In an experiment trial, we create true model ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}, which is an
instantiation of a canonical SCM with random parameters. Unlike cases where ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} comes
from a set family of SCMs, which may produce biased results, the expressiveness of the canonical
SCM allows us to robustly sample data generating models that can take any behavior consistent with
the constraints of 𝒢\mathcal{G}.

In the estimation experiments, to make things more challenging, we take one additional step after
constructing ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} in cases where P⁡(Y∣d​o​(X))P(Y\mid do(X)) is not necessarily equal
to P⁡(Y∣X)P(Y\mid X). To ensure that this inequality holds, we widen the difference between ATE and
total variation (TV), computed in this case as P⁡(Y=1∣X=1)−P⁡(Y=1∣X=0)P(Y=1\mid X=1)-P(Y=1\mid X=
0). We accomplish this by performing gradient descent on the parameters of the canonical SCM until
this difference is at least 0.05.

From the perspective of simulating the ground truth, sampling from the canonical SCM can be
accomplished using the same procedure from Def. 2.

B.2 Identification Running Procedure

For the identification experiments, we test on the 8 graphs shown in the top of Fig. 4. For each
graph, we run 20 trials, each repeated 4 times on the same dataset for the sake of hypothesis
testing.

For each trial, we create a canonical SCM from the graph 𝒢\mathcal{G} for the data generating model
ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}. We sample 10,000 data points from P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}), where 𝐕\mathbf{V} is
determined by the nodes in 𝒢\mathcal{G}, each being a binary variable.

With 𝒢\mathcal{G} as input, we instantiate two NCMs, M^min\widehat{M}_{\min} and M^max\widehat{M}_
{\max}, and train them on the given data using a PyTorch Lightning trainer. Both models are trained
for 3000 epochs using the minimization and maximization version of the objective in Eq. 5,
respectively. λ\lambda is initialized to 1 at the beginning of training and decreases
logarithmically throughout training until it reaches 0.001 at the end.

We use the AdamW optimizer (Loshchilov and Hutter,, 2019) with the Cosine Annealing Warm Restarts
learning rate scheduler (Loshchilov and Hutter,, 2017). When using Eq. 3 to compute probabilities,
we choose m=20000m=20000.

We log the ATE of both models every 10 iterations and use it to compute the max-min gaps from the
l.h.s. of Eq. 6. The gaps from each trial are averaged across the 4 runs. The percentiles of these
gaps over the 20 trials are plotted across the 3000 epochs in the bottom row of Fig. 4. We use
these gaps in the hypothesis testing procedure described later in Sec. B.4 with τ=0.01,0.03,0.05\
tau=0.01,0.03,0.05 and plot the accuracy over the 20 trials in the middle row of Fig. 4. For
smoother plots, we use the running average over 50 iterations.

To ensure reproducibility, each run’s random seed is generated through a SHA512 encoding of a key.
The key is determined by the parameters of the trial such as the graph, the number of samples, and
the trial index.

The NCMs are trained on NVIDIA Tesla V100 GPUs provided by Amazon Web Services. In total, we used
about 150 GPU hours for the identification experiments.

B.3 Estimation Running Procedure

For the estimation experiments, we test on the 4 identifiable graphs at the top of Fig. 4. For each
graph, we test 15 different amounts of sample data, ranging from 10310^{3} to 10610^{6} on a
logarithmic scale. Each setting is run for 25 trials.

For each trial, we create a canonical SCM from the graph 𝒢\mathcal{G} for the data generating model
ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}. We sample the data from P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}), where 𝐕\mathbf{V} is determined by
the nodes in 𝒢\mathcal{G}, each being a binary variable.

We instantiate an NCM, M^\widehat{M}, given 𝒢\mathcal{G}, and we train it on the given data using a
PyTorch Lightning trainer. The model is trained using the objective in Eq. 4. We incorporate early
stopping, where the model ends training if loss does not decrease by a minimum of 10−610^{-6}
within 100 epochs. After training, the parameters of the best model encountered during training are
reloaded.

We use the AdamW optimizer (Loshchilov and Hutter,, 2019) with the Cosine Annealing Warm Restarts
learning rate scheduler (Loshchilov and Hutter,, 2017). When using Eq. 3 to compute probabilities,
we choose m=1.6×106m=1.6\times 10^{6}.

For each trial, we save the training data and perform the same estimation experiment with the naïve
likelihood maximization model and WERM Jung et al., 2020b (). The naïve model is also optimized
with the objective in Eq. 4 to fit P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}). Since code for WERM is not available, we
implement our own version in Python trying to match the code provided in the paper. Notably, we use
XGBoost (Chen and Guestrin,, 2016) regressors trained on the data to learn the WERM weights. These
regressors are chosen with 20 estimators and a max depth of 10. They are trained with the binary
logistic objective for binary outputs, otherwise they are trained using the squared error
objective. The regularization parameter λWERM\lambda_{\text{WERM}} (not to be confused with the λ\
lambda in Eq. 5) is learned through hyperparameter tuning, checking every value from 0 to 10 in
increments of 0.2. The regularization parameter αWERM\alpha_{\text{WERM}} is set to λWERM/2\lambda_
{\text{WERM}}/2.

We record the KL divergence of the best models of the NCM and naïve models after training, computed
as

 DKL(Pℳ∗(𝐕)||PM^(𝐕))=∑𝐯∈𝒟𝐕Pℳ∗(𝐯)log(Pℳ∗​(𝐯)PM^​(𝐯)).D_{\kl}\left(P^{\mathcal{M}^{*}}(\mathbf{V})||
 P^{\widehat{M}}(\mathbf{V})\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{V}}}P^{\mathcal{M}^
 {*}}(\mathbf{v})\log\left(\frac{P^{\mathcal{M}^{*}}(\mathbf{v})}{P^{\widehat{M}}(\mathbf{v})}\
 right).

The mean and 95%-confidence intervals of the KL divergence across the 25 trials are plotted over
each setting of sample size in the top row of Fig. 5.

We also record the ATE computed from all three methods after training. In the case of the naïve
model, we use TV as the ATE calculation. For the NCM and naïve model, we use the sampling procedure
described in Eq. 3 with m=106m=10^{6} to compute these results. The mean and 95%-confidence
intervals of the ATE computations across the 25 trials are plotted over each setting of sample size
in the bottom row of Fig. 5.

Randomization is performed similarly to the identification experiments.

The NCMs and naïve models are trained on NVIDIA Tesla V100 GPUs provided by Amazon Web Services. In
total, we used about 300 GPU hours for the estimation experiments.

B.4 Identification Hypothesis Testing

We incorporate a basic hypothesis testing procedure to test the inequality in Eq. 6. In each trial
of the identification experiments, we rerun the procedure on the same dataset rr times (r=4r=4 in
our experiments). Let Δ​Q^\Delta\widehat{Q} denote the random variable representing the max-min gap
from a run in a specific trial, and let {Δ​q^i}i=1r\{\Delta\hat{q}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r} denote the
empirical set of max-min gaps from all rr runs. The randomness in Δ​Q^\Delta\widehat{Q} arises from
the randomness of parameter initialization in the NCM. Δ​Q^\Delta\widehat{Q} is not necessarily
normally distributed, but we assume that the mean of {Δ​q^i}i=1r\{\Delta\hat{q}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
will be normally distributed given a large enough rr due to the central limit theorem.

For a given τ\tau, we test the hypothesis that 𝔼⁡[Δ​Q^]<τ\mathbb{E}[\Delta\widehat{Q}]<\tau with
the null hypothesis that 𝔼⁡[Δ​Q^]≥τ\mathbb{E}[\Delta\widehat{Q}]\geq\tau. We estimate

 𝔼⁡[Δ​Q^]≈Δ​q^¯:=1r​∑i=1rΔ​q^i,\mathbb{E}[\Delta\widehat{Q}]\approx\overline{\Delta\hat{q}}:=\frac
 {1}{r}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\Delta\hat{q}_{i},

the mean of the empirical set of gaps. Then we compute the standard error

 SE⁡(Δ​q^):=1r​∑i=1r(Δ​q^i−Δ​q^¯).\se(\Delta\hat{q}):=\frac{1}{r}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{r}\left(\Delta\
 hat{q}_{i}-\overline{\Delta\hat{q}}\right)}.

Refer to caption
Figure 7: Example plots aggregating max-min gaps across multiple settings. Left is ID, right is
nonID. Bottom plots are zoomed versions of top plots. Dashed lines represent choices of τ\tau at
0.01 (red), 0.03 (green) and 0.05 (blue).

Finally, we check

 Δ​q^¯+1.65​SE⁡(Δ​q^)<τ.\overline{\Delta\hat{q}}+1.65\se(\Delta\hat{q})<\tau.

If this quantity holds, we reject the null hypothesis with 95% confidence, and we return the result
that the query is identifiable. If not, we fail to reject the null hypothesis and we return the
result that the query is not identifiable.

Refer to caption
Figure 8: NCM estimation results for ID graphs when setting the number of observed samples to
1000000 and the number of dimensions for each multi-dimensional binary covariate (any variable
which is not XX or YY) to 20. The graphs displayed in the plot correspond to the same graphs as a,
b, c, d in Fig. 4. Plot in log scale.

We showed the results of three different values of τ\tau (0.01,0.03,0.050.01,0.03,0.05) in Fig. 4
(main paper). Here, we try to understand how τ\tau can be determined more systematically by running
a preliminary round of identification experiments and observing the percentiles of the resulting
gaps; see Fig. 7. Note that most gaps in the ID case fall below the 0.030.03 line by the end of
training process, while most gaps in the nonID case stay above it. This can be used to motivate a
particular choice of τ\tau.

B.5 Additional Results

To demonstrate the potential of NCMs to scale to larger settings, we provide additional estimation
results on higher dimensional data in Fig. 8. We use the same canonical SCM architecture but
probabilistically map the value of each binary variable which is not XX or YY to a 20-dimensional
binary vector, such that there exists a deterministic mapping from each covariate’s 20-dimensional
binary vectors to the original binary value (that is, the original binary value is recoverable from
the 20-dimensional mapped vector). This preserves ATE and TV while increasing the difficulty of the
NCM’s optimization problem by testing the NCM’s ability to learn in high-dimensional spaces. In
this more challenging high-dimensional setting, the NCM maintains superior or equal performance
when compared to WERM.

B.6 Other Possible Architectures

In Sec. 4, we provided one possible architecture of the NCM (Eq. 2), which produced the empirical
results discussed in Sec. 5. These results show that, in principle, training a proxy model can be
used to solve the identification and estimation problems in practice.

Still, other architectures may be more suitable and perform more efficiently than the one shown in
Eq. 2 in larger, more involved domains. In such settings, it may be challenging to match the
distributions well enough to reach the correct identification result, or to lower the error on
estimating the query of interest. While Corol. 4 guarantees the correct result with perfect
optimization, this is rarely achieved in practice. It’s certainly not fully understood how
optimization errors on the learning of P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}) will affect the performance of
identification/estimation of Layer 2 quantities.

Naturally, the framework developed here to support proxy SCMs is not limited to the architecture in
Eq. 2. For instance, as discussed in Footnote 9, there are alternative methods of learning P⁡(𝐕)P(\
mathbf{V}) such as by minimizing divergences, performing variational inference, generative
adversarial optimization, to cite a few prominent choices. Some of these methods may be more
scalable and robust to optimization errors. See Appendix D.1 for a discussion on how the theory
generalizes for other architectures.

Further, we note that if 𝐕\mathbf{V} comes from continuous domains, there are many ways the
architecture of the NCM could be augmented accordingly. As discussed in Footnote 10, one could
replace the Gumbel-max trick in the architecture in Eq. 2 with a model that directly computes a
probability density given a data point. Some of the alternative architectures mentioned above may
work as well.

We believe this leads to an exciting research agenda that is to understand the tradeoffs involved
in the choice of the architectures and how to properly optimize NCMs for specific applications.

Appendix C Examples and Further Discussion

In this section, we complement the discussion provided in the main paper through examples.
Specifically, in Sec. C.1, we discuss what expressiveness means and provide examples to show why
it’s insufficient to perform cross-layer inferences. In Sec. C.2, we provide an intuitive
discussion on why causal diagrams are able to perform cross-layer inferences and basic examples
illustrating the structural constraints encoded in such models, which can be seen as an inductive
bias as discussed in the body of the paper. In Sec. C.3, we exemplify how this inductive bias can
be added to NCMs such that they are capable of solving ID instances. In Sec. C.4, we discuss
possible reasons why one would prefer to perform ID through neural computation instead the
machinery of the do-calculus.

C.1 Expressiveness Examples

In this section, we try to clarify through examples the notion of expressiveness, as shown in Thm.
1, and discuss the reasons it alone is not sufficient for performing cross-layer inferences.

1.

We state the following useful neural network functions defined in the proof of Thm. 1:

  • •
   
    f^AND​(x1,…,xn)=σ⁡(∑i=1nxi−n)\hat{f}_{\scand}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\sigma\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_

For this appendix, σ\sigma will be the binary step activation function. f^AND\hat{f}_{\scand}, f^OR
\hat{f}_{\scor}, and f^NOT\hat{f}_{\scnot} compute the bitwise AND, OR, and NOT of the inputs (if
binary) respectively. f^≥z\hat{f}_{\geq z} will output 1 if its input is greater than or equal to
zz and will output 0 otherwise.

We begin the construction of our NCM M^=⟨𝐔^,𝐕,ℱ^,P(𝐔^)\widehat{M}=\langle\widehat{\mathbf{U}},\

With f^D\hat{f}_{D} and f^B\hat{f}_{B} defined, our construction of M^\widehat{M} is complete.

We can now verify that the distributions induced by M^\widehat{M}, as represented in Table 2,
matches the distributions induced by ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}. Consider the same three queries from Eqs.
25, 27, and 29:

 PM^​(B=1∣D=1)=PM^​(D=1,B=1)PM^​(D=1)=q4q4+q5=54144=0.375P^{\widehat{M}}(B=1\mid D=1)=\frac{P^{\
 widehat{M}}(D=1,B=1)}{P^{\widehat{M}}(D=1)}=\frac{q_{4}}{q_{4}+q_{5}}=\frac{54}{144}=0.375

 PM^​(B=1∣d​o​(D=1))=q1+q3+q4=120256=0.46875P^{\widehat{M}}(B=1\mid do(D=1))=q_{1}+q_{3}+q_{4}=\
 frac{120}{256}=0.46875

  PM^​(BD=1=1∣D=0,B=1)\displaystyle P^{\widehat{M}}(B_{D=1}=1\mid D=0,B=1)
  =PM^​(BD=1=1,D=0,B=1)PM^​(D=0,B=1)=q3q2+q3=4878≈0.6154\displaystyle=\frac{P^{\widehat{M}}(B_{D=
  1}=1,D=0,B=1)}{P^{\widehat{M}}(D=0,B=1)}=\frac{q_{3}}{q_{2}+q_{3}}=\frac{48}{78}\approx 0.6154

This demonstrates that the NCM is indeed expressive enough to model this setting on all three
layers of PCH. While a more basic model might be fitted to answer L1L_{1} queries (such as P⁡(B=1∣D
=1)P(B=1\mid D=1)), a well-parameterized NCM is expressive enough to represent distributions on
higher layers. ■\blacksquare

Example 1 shows a scenario where the expressiveness of the NCM class allowed us to fit an NCM
instance that could completely match a complex SCM and answer any question of interest. However, we
also highlight that expressiveness alone does not necessarily mean that the NCM can solve any
causal problem. Experimental data, data from layer 2, can be difficult to obtain in practice, so it
would be convenient if we could fit a model M^\widehat{M} only on observational data from layer 1,
then deduce the same causal results using M^\widehat{M} as if it were the true SCM, ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^
{*}. Unfortunately, Corol. 1 states that this deduction typically cannot be made no matter the
expressiveness of the model. The next example will illustrate this point more concretely.

2.

DD BB P⁡(D,B)P(D,B)
0  0  34/25634/256
0  1  78/25678/256
1  0  90/25690/256
1  1  54/25654/256


Table 3: Observational distribution P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}) induced by ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} from Example 1.

Consider the same study of the effects of diet on blood pressure in Example 1 in which the two
variables are described by the SCM, ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}, in Eq. 24.

While in the previous example we were able to construct NCM M^\widehat{M} which matched ℳ∗\mathcal
{M}^{*}’s behavior on all three layers of the PCH, this was contingent on having access to the true
SCM. This is unlikely to happen in practice. Instead, we typically only have partial information
from the SCM. In many cases, we may only have observational data from layer 1. The data in Table 3
exemplifies the aspect of ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} we may have access to.

Naturally, we should construct an NCM such that matches on the given observed dataset. The NCM M^\
widehat{M} from Example 1 will indeed induce the same observational data P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}), but
there are several other ways we could construct a model that fits such criterion. For illustration
purposes, consider four NCMs M^1\widehat{M}_{1}, M^2\widehat{M}_{2}, M^3\widehat{M}_{3}, and M^4\
widehat{M}_{4} such that M^i=⟨𝐔^,𝐕,ℱ^i,P⁡(𝐔^)⟩\widehat{M}_{i}=\langle\widehat{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf
{V},\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{i},P(\widehat{\mathbf{U}})\rangle is defined as follows:

 {𝐔^:={U^D,U^B},𝒟U^D=𝒟U^B=[0,1]𝐕:={D,B}ℱ^i:={f^Di,f^Bi}P⁡(𝐔^):=U^D,U^B∼Unif(0,1),U^D⟂⁣⟂U^B\begin
 {cases}\widehat{\mathbf{U}}&:=\{\widehat{U}_{D},\widehat{U}_{B}\},\mathcal{D}_{\widehat{U}_{D}}=\
 mathcal{D}_{\widehat{U}_{B}}=[0,1]\\ \mathbf{V}&:=\{D,B\}\\ \widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{i}&:=\{\hat{f}
 _{D}^{i},\hat{f}_{B}^{i}\}\\ P(\widehat{\mathbf{U}})&:=\widehat{U}_{D},\widehat{U}_{B}\sim\unif
 (0,1),\widehat{U}_{D}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern
 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\

Even though the functions of these NCMs are constructed in very different ways, one can verify that
they indeed induce the same layer 1 as shown in Table 3.

Despite this match, they all disagree on a simple layer 2 quantity such as P⁡(B=1∣d​o​(D=1))P(B=1\
mid do(D=1)); to witness note that

Without further information, it’s not possible to distinguish which model (if any) is the correct
one. Naïevely, choosing an arbitrary model is likely to result in misleading results, even if the
model can reproduce the given L1L_{1} data with high fidelity.

In practice, depending on the chosen model, the conclusion of the study could be dramatically
different. For instance, someone who fits models M^1\widehat{M}_{1} or M^4\widehat{M}_{4} may
conclude that a high-vegetable diet is beneficial for lowering blood pressure, while someone who
fits model M^2\widehat{M}_{2} may conclude that it has no effect at all. Someone who fits model M^3
\widehat{M}_{3} may even conclude that eating more vegetables is harmful for regulating blood
pressure.

■\blacksquare

C.2 Structural Constraints Embedded in Causal Bayesian Networks

In this section, we provide an example to illustrate the inductive bias embedded in causal
diagrams, building on the more comprehensive treatment provided in (Bareinboim et al.,, 2020, Sec.
1.4).

3.

Consider the following two SCMs:

 ℳ1:={𝐔:={UX,UY}, all binary𝐕:={X,Y}, all binaryℱ1:={fX1​(UX)=UXfY1​(X,UY)=X⊕UYP⁡(𝐔):=P(UX=1)=12,P
 (UY=1)=14,UX⟂⁣⟂UY\mathcal{M}_{1}:=\begin{cases}\mathbf{U}&:=\{U_{X},U_{Y}\},\text{ all binary}\\
 \mathbf{V}&:=\{X,Y\},\text{ all binary}\\ \mathcal{F}_{1}&:=\begin{cases}f^{1}_{X}(U_{X})&=U_{X}\
 \ f^{1}_{Y}(X,U_{Y})&=X\oplus U_{Y}\end{cases}\\ P(\mathbf{U})&:=P(U_{X}=1)=\frac{1}{2},P(U_{Y}=
 1)=\frac{1}{4},U_{X}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern
 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\
 hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\
 perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\
 scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}U_
 {Y}\end{cases}

 ℳ2:={𝐔:={UX,UY}, all binary𝐕:={X,Y}, all binaryℱ2:={fX2​(Y,UX)=Y⊕UXfY2​(UY)=UYP⁡(𝐔):=P(UX=1)=14,P
 (UY=1)=12,UX⟂⁣⟂UY\mathcal{M}_{2}:=\begin{cases}\mathbf{U}&:=\{U_{X},U_{Y}\},\text{ all binary}\\
 \mathbf{V}&:=\{X,Y\},\text{ all binary}\\ \mathcal{F}_{2}&:=\begin{cases}f^{2}_{X}(Y,U_{X})&=Y\
 oplus U_{X}\\ f^{2}_{Y}(U_{Y})&=U_{Y}\end{cases}\\ P(\mathbf{U})&:=P(U_{X}=1)=\frac{1}{4},P(U_{Y}
 =1)=\frac{1}{2},U_{X}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern
 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\
 hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\
 perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\
 scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}U_
 {Y}\end{cases}

Note that both ℳ1\mathcal{M}_{1} and ℳ2\mathcal{M}_{2} induce the same L1L_{1} distributions.

For example, in both models, P⁡(Y=1)=12P(Y=1)=\frac{1}{2}, and P⁡(Y=1∣X=1)=34P(Y=1\mid X=1)=\frac
{3}{4}. However, even without making any computation, it seems intuitive from the structure of the
functions and noise that a causal query like P⁡(Y=1∣d​o​(X=1))P(Y=1\mid do(X=1)) would have
different answers in both models. First, note that fY2f^{2}_{Y} in ℳ2\mathcal{M}_{2} does not have
XX as an argument, and there are no other variables in 𝐕\mathbf{V}, so intervening on XX would have
no causal effect on YY. In other words, the constraint Pℳ2​(Y=1∣d​o​(X=1))=P⁡(Y=1)P^{\mathcal{M}_
{2}}(Y=1\mid do(X=1))=P(Y=1) is implied. More subtly in the case of ℳ1\mathcal{M}_{1}, we note that
XX directly affects YY and there is no unobserved confounding between XX and YY. This means that
the observed association between XX and YY must be due to the causal effect (i.e. Pℳ1​(Y=1∣d​o​(X=
1))=P⁡(Y=1∣X=1)P^{\mathcal{M}_{1}}(Y=1\mid do(X=1))=P(Y=1\mid X=1)). The relationship between XX
and YY in these two cases can be seen graphically, as shown in Fig. 9.

XXYY
(a) Graph for ℳ1\mathcal{M}_{1}.

XXYY
(b) Graph for ℳ2\mathcal{M}_{2}.


Figure 9: Causal diagrams for SCMs in Example 3. A directed edge from AA to BB indicates that AA is
an argument of the function for BB.

■\blacksquare

As illustrated in the previous example, and discussed more formally in (Bareinboim et al.,, 2020),
these equality constraints across distributions arise from the qualitative structural properties of
the SCM such as which variable is an argument of which function, and which variables share
exogenous influence. In fact, these constraints are invariant to the details of the functions and
the distribution of the exogenous variables.

Moreover, there are exponentially many constraints implied by an SCM in the collection of L1L_{1}
and L2L_{2} distributions, which can be parsimoniously represented in the form of a causal bayesian
network (Def. 15)^12^12 12 This is a generalization of the notion of markov compatibility used in
L1L_{1} models, such as bayesian networks Pearl, (1988). In our case, there are multiple
distributions of different nature, observational and experimental, and the constraints are among
them (i.e., not conditional independences). In fact, the graphical component of this object
provides an intuitive interpretation for these constraints. It can be shown that the closure of
such constraints entails the do-calculus (Bareinboim et al.,, 2020, Thm. 5), which means that all
identifiable effects can be computed from them (due to the completeness shown in Lee et al., (2019)
).

DDBB
Figure 10: Causal diagram 𝒢\mathcal{G} of ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} from Example 1

Revisiting Examples 1 and 2 and applying the definition of causal diagram (Def. 12), we can see
from the structure of ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} that the causal diagram 𝒢\mathcal{G} in Fig. 10 is induced.
If we had this information, we could immediately eliminate M^2\widehat{M}_{2} from Example 2 as a
possible model. The reason is that we can read off from diagram 𝒢\mathcal{G} that BB cannot be an
argument of fDf_{D}.

With an SCM-like structure, the NCM naturally induces its own structural constraints. Def. 7 in the
main paper shows how to construct an NCM to fit the same constraints entailed by a given causal
diagram from another SCM. In some way, the original model induces mark in the collection of
observational and experimental distributions. In the case of the NCM, these constraints are used as
input, inductive bias to constrain its functions. This is powerful because any inferences performed
on a 𝒢\mathcal{G}-constrained NCM will automatically satisfy the constraints of 𝒢\mathcal{G}.

C.3 Solving Identification through NCMs

Refer to caption
(a) In the identifiable case, all NCMs that are 𝒢\mathcal{G}-consistent and L1L_{1}-consistent with
ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} will also match in QQ.

Refer to caption
(b) In the non-identifiable case, there could exist two NCMs, M^1\widehat{M}_{1} and M^2\widehat{M}
_{2}, that are both 𝒢\mathcal{G}-consistent and L1L_{1}-consistent but still disagree in QQ.


Figure 11: A visual representation of the ID problem. Here, QQ is a query of interest, and Q^i\
widehat{Q}_{i} is the answer for that query induced by NCM M^i\widehat{M}_{i}. The goal is to check
if all NCMs that are 𝒢\mathcal{G}-consistent and L1L_{1}-consistent are also consistent in QQ.

The notion of identification requires that a certain effect is identifiable by all (unobserved)
SCMs compatible with the corresponding structural constraints. This was extended to NCMs through
Def. 8; see also Fig. 11. In fact, Alg. 1 helps to solve the neural identification problem such
that if there are two NCMs compatible with the constraints but with different predictions for the
causal effect, it will return “FAIL”. Otherwise, it will returns the estimation of the query in
every ID case. Note that identification of an effect does not require that the NCM and the true SCM
have the same functions, just the effects need to match. The following two examples illustrate both
positive and negative instances of such operation.

4.

Consider once again the study of diet on blood pressure introduced in Example 1. Suppose we are
particularly interested in the L2L_{2} expression of Q=P⁡(B=1∣d​o​(D=1))Q=P(B=1\mid do(D=1)), the
causal effect of diet on blood pressure. The true SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} is not available, but L1L_
{1} data (P⁡(𝐯)P(\mathbf{v})) presented in Table 3 is (as shown Example 2). Furthermore, we are
informed by a doctor that the two variables follow the relation specified by the causal diagram 𝒢\
mathcal{G} in Fig. 10. Is QQ identifiable from P⁡(𝐯)P(\mathbf{v}) and Ω⁡(𝒢)\Omega(\mathcal{G}),
where Ω⁡(𝒢)\Omega(\mathcal{G}) is the set of all 𝒢\mathcal{G}-constrained NCMs?

Unfortunately, the answer is no for this particular case. To see why, example 2 provides 4 NCMs
whose L1L_{1} distributions match P⁡(𝐯)P(\mathbf{v}) but disagree on QQ. Even though M^2\widehat{M}
_{2} can be eliminated as a possible proxy since it is not 𝒢\mathcal{G}-consistent, the other three
models are compatible with 𝒢\mathcal{G}, so we still cannot pinpoint the correct answer for QQ.

Running Alg. 1 on this setting would result in the algorithm generating two sets of parameters,
𝛉min∗\bm{\theta}^{*}_{\min} and 𝛉max∗\bm{\theta}^{*}_{\max}, that minimize and maximize QQ. The
behaviors of an NCM with these parameters may reflect the behaviors of M^3\widehat{M}_{3} and M^4\
widehat{M}_{4} from Example 2, and they do not agree on QQ. ■\blacksquare

5.

Suppose now we return to the data collection process and we receive new information about a third
variable, sodium intake (SS) (S=1S=1 represents a high sodium diet, 00 otherwise).

With the introduction of SS in the endogenous set 𝐕\mathbf{V}, ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} can be written as
follows:

 ℳ∗:={𝐔:={UD,UD​B,US,UB}, all binary𝐕:={D,S,B}ℱ:={fD​(UD,UD​B)=¬UD∧¬UD​BfS​(D,US)=¬D⊕USfB​
 (S,UB)=(S∨UD​B)⊕UBP⁡(𝐔):={P⁡(UD=1)=P⁡(UD​B=1)=P⁡(US=1)=P⁡(UB=1)=14 all ​U∈𝐔​ are independent
 \mathcal{M}^{*}:=\begin{cases}\mathbf{U}&:=\{U_{D},U_{DB},U_{S},U_{B}\},\text{ all binary}\\
 \mathbf{V}&:=\{D,S,B\}\\ \mathcal{F}&:=\begin{cases}f_{D}(U_{D},U_{DB})&=\neg U_{D}\wedge\    (54)
 neg U_{DB}\\ f_{S}(D,U_{S})&=\neg D\oplus U_{S}\\ f_{B}(S,U_{B})&=(S\vee U_{DB})\oplus U_{B}
 \end{cases}\\ P(\mathbf{U})&:=\begin{cases}P(U_{D}=1)=P(U_{DB}=1)=P(U_{S}=1)=P(U_{B}=1)=\
 frac{1}{4}\\ \text{ all }U\in\mathbf{U}\text{ are independent}\end{cases}\end{cases}

As reflected in the updated ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}, diet only affects blood pressure through sodium
content, which tends to be higher in low-vegetable diets. Note that the L1L_{1}, L2L_{2}, and L3L_
{3} distributions relating DD and BB are unchanged with this modification of ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}. In
this case, the query of interest is evaluated as

 P⁡(B=1∣d​o​(D=1))\displaystyle =P⁡((SD=1∨UD​B)⊕UB=1)\displaystyle=P\left((S_{D=1}\vee U_
 P(B=1\mid do(D=1))             {DB})\oplus U_{B}=1\right)
                                =P⁡(((¬1⊕US)∨UD​B)⊕UB=1)\displaystyle=P\left(((\neg 1\oplus
                                U_{S})\vee U_{DB})\oplus U_{B}=1\right)
                                =P⁡((US∨UD​B)⊕UB=1)\displaystyle=P\left((U_{S}\vee U_{DB})\
                                oplus U_{B}=1\right)
                                =120256=0.46875,\displaystyle=\frac{120}{256}=0.46875,         (55)

which matches the result in Eq. 27.

This is just the computation of the distribution from Nature’s perspective. As the previous
examples, we do not have access to the true model (ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}), just the observational
distribution P⁡(𝐯)P(\mathbf{v}), as shown in Table 4. We do have access to the updated causal
diagram as shown in Fig. 12.

The addition of the new variable and the refinement of the model change the identifiability status
of the query Q=P⁡(B=1∣d​o​(D=1))Q=P(B=1\mid do(D=1)). No matter how Alg. 1 chooses the parameters
for 𝛉min∗\bm{\theta}^{*}_{\min} and 𝛉max∗\bm{\theta}^{*}_{\max}, M^\widehat{M} will always induce
the same result for QQ as long as it induces the correct P⁡(𝐯)P(\mathbf{v}). Specifically, through
standard do-calculus derivation we would obtain:

This result indeed matches Eq. 55. We further note M^\widehat{M} is constructed to fit P⁡(𝐯)P(\
mathbf{v}) and not necessarily to match ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}. This is evident when comparing the
inputs and outputs of the functions in ℱ^\widehat{\mathcal{F}} to those from ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}.
Still, due to the structural constraints encoded in the NCM, it so happens that M^\widehat{M} also
matches in our L2L_{2} query of interest even though it is only constructed to match only on layer
1. It is remarkable that incorporating these family of constraints into the NCM structure allows
one to successfully perform cross-layer inferences.

■\blacksquare

Thm. 4 is powerful because it says that performing the identification task in the class of NCMs
will yield the correct result, even if the true model can be any SCM. We note that this is not the
case for every class of models, even if a model from that class can be both L1L_{1}-consistent and
𝒢\mathcal{G}-consistent with the true model. In particular, the expressivity of the NCM allows for
this result, and using a less expressive model may produce incorrect results. We illustrate this
with the following examples.

6.

Suppose we attempt to identify P⁡(B=1∣d​o​(D=1))P(B=1\mid do(D=1)) from the problem in Example 4
using a less expressive model class such as the set of all Markovian models. Recall that the given
observational P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}) is shown in Table 3, and the given causal diagram 𝒢\mathcal{G} is
shown in Fig. 10. An example model from the class of Markovian models that achieve L1L_{1} and 𝒢\
mathcal{G} consistency with ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} is M^1\widehat{M}_{1} from Example 2.

Note that due to the lack of unobserved confounding in Markovian models, the induced value for P⁡(B
=1∣d​o​(D=1))P(B=1\mid do(D=1)) for any L1L_{1} and 𝒢\mathcal{G}-consistent Markovian model
including M^1\widehat{M}_{1} is P⁡(B=1∣d​o​(D=1))=P⁡(B=1∣D=1)=54144=0.375P(B=1\mid do(D=1))=P(B=1\
mid D=1)=\frac{54}{144}=0.375. Since all models from this class (Markovian) agree on the same value
for this quantity, the conclusion reached is that it must be identifiable. However, this is clearly
not the case, as we know the true value, Pℳ∗​(B=1∣d​o​(D=1))=0.46875P^{\mathcal{M}^{*}}(B=1\mid do
(D=1))=0.46875. In this case, the reason we reach the wrong result is that the true model is not
Markovian, and the set of all Markovian models is not expressive enough to account for all possible
SCMs. ■\blacksquare

More interestingly, another example using a different class of models follows.

7.

Consider a special class of SCMs which, given variables 𝐕\mathbf{V} and the graph 𝒢\mathcal{G},
take the following structure.

Simply put, ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} could be an SCM for which the model class in Eq. 61 is not expressive
enough to capture.

■\blacksquare

C.4 Symbolic versus Optimization-based Approaches for Identification

In this section, we discuss the relationship between current approaches to causal identification/
estimation versus the new, neural/optimization-based approach proposed in this work.

The problem of effect identification has been extensively studied in the literature, and (Pearl,,
1995) introduced the do-calculus (akin to differential or integral calculus), a set of symbolic
rules that can be applied to any expression and evaluate whether a certain invariance across
interventional distributions hold given local assumptions encoded in a causal diagram. Multiple
applications of the rules can be combined to search for a reduction from a target effect to the
distributions in which data is available. There exist algorithms capable of utilizing the
structural constraints encoded in the causal diagram, based on what is known as c-component
factorization (Tian and Pearl,, 2002), to find a symbolic expression of the L2L_{2} query in terms
of the L1L_{1} distribution efficiently (for more general tasks, see, e.g., Lee et al., (2019);
Correa and Bareinboim, (2020)). We call this approach symbolic since it aims to find a closed-form
expression of the target effect in terms of the input distributions.

Alternatively, one could take an optimization-based route to tackle the identification problem,
such as in Alg. 1 discussed in Sec. 3. This approach entails a search through the space of possible
structural models while trying to maximize/minimize the value of the target query subject to the
constraints found in the inputted data. We call this an optimization-based approach. These two
approaches are indeed linked as evident from Thm. 2, which establishes a duality saying that the
identification status of a target query is shared across symbolic and optimization-based
approaches.

We discuss next some of the possible trade-offs and synergies between these two families of
methods. We start with the symbolic approach and re-stating the definition of identification (
Pearl,, 2000), with minor modifications to highlight its model-theoretic perspective regarding the
space of SCMs:

17Causal Effect Identification.

Let Ω∗\Omega^{*} be the space containing all SCMs defined over endogenous variables 𝐕\mathbf{V}. We
say that a causal effect P⁡(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))P(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x})) is identifiable from the
observational distribution P⁡(𝐯)P(\mathbf{v}) and the causal diagram 𝒢\mathcal{G} if Pℳ1​(𝐲∣d​o​
(𝐱))=Pℳ2​(𝐲∣d​o​(𝐱))P^{\mathcal{M}_{1}}(\mathbf{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x}))=P^{\mathcal{M}_{2}}(\mathbf
{y}\mid do(\mathbf{x})) for every pair of models ℳ1,ℳ2∈Ω∗\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2}\in\Omega^
{*} such that ℳ1\mathcal{M}_{1} and ℳ2\mathcal{M}_{2} both induce 𝒢\mathcal{G} and Pℳ1​(𝐯)=Pℳ2​(𝐯)P
^{\mathcal{M}_{1}}(\mathbf{v})=P^{\mathcal{M}_{2}}(\mathbf{v}) ■\blacksquare

𝛀∗\bm{\Omega}^{*}ℳ1\mathcal{M}^{1} ℳ2\mathcal{M}^{2}(ℒ1\mathcal{L}_{1})ObservationalDistributions
DataPℳ1​(𝐕)=Pℳ2​(𝐕)P^{\mathcal{M}_{1}}(\mathbf{V}){=}P^{\mathcal{M}_{2}}(\mathbf{V}) (ℒ2\mathcal{L}
_{2})InterventionalDistributionsQueryPℳ1​(𝐘|d​o​(𝐱))=P^{\mathcal{M}_{1}}(\mathbf{Y}|do(\mathbf{x}))
=Pℳ2​(𝐘|d​o​(𝐱))P^{\mathcal{M}_{2}}(\mathbf{Y}|do(\mathbf{x})) ?Structural ConstraintsCausal
Diagram𝒢\mathcal{G}
Figure 14: P⁡(𝐘∣d​o​(𝐱))P(\mathbf{Y}\mid do(\mathbf{x})) is identifiable from P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V})
and 𝒢\mathcal{G} if for all SCM ℳ1,ℳ2\mathcal{M}^{1},\mathcal{M}^{2} (top left) such that ℳ1,ℳ2\
mathcal{M}^{1},\mathcal{M}^{2} match in P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}) (bottom left) and 𝒢\mathcal{G} (top
right), then they also match in the target distribution P⁡(𝐘∣d​o​(𝐱))P(\mathbf{Y}\mid do(\mathbf
{x})) (bottom right).

Fig. 14 provides an illustration of the many parts involved in this definition. In words, an
interventional distribution Q=P⁡(𝐘∣d​o​(𝐱))Q=P(\mathbf{Y}\mid do(\mathbf{x})) is said to be
identifiable if for all SCMs in Ω∗\Omega^{*} (top-left) that share the same causal diagram 𝒢\
mathcal{G} (top-right), and induce the same probability distribution P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V})
(bottom-left), they generate the same distribution to the target query QQ (bottom-right). In fact,
identifiability can be understood as if the details of the specific form of the true SCM ℳ∗\mathcal
{M}^{*} – its functions and probability distribution over the exogenous variables – are irrelevant,
and the constraints encoded in the causal diagram are sufficient to perform the intended
cross-layer inference from the source to the target distributions.

One important observation that follows is that, operationally, symbolic methods do not work
directly in the Ω∗\Omega^{*} space, but on top of the constraints implied by the true SCM on the
causal diagram. There are a number of reasons for this, but we note that if all that is available
about ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} are the constraints encoded in 𝒢\mathcal{G}, it is somewhat expected and
reasonable to operate directly on those, instead of considering the elusive, underlying structural
causal model. Further, we already know through the CHT that we will never be able to recover ℳ∗\
mathcal{M}^{*} anyways, so one could see it as unreasonable to consider ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} as the
target of the analysis. Still, even if we wanted to refer directly to ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*},
practically speaking, searching through the space Ω∗\Omega^{*} is a daunting task since each
candidate SCM ℳ=⟨ℱ,P⁡(𝐔)⟩∈Ω∗\mathcal{M}=\langle\mathcal{F},P(\mathbf{U})\rangle\in\Omega^{*} is a
complex, infinite dimensional object. Note that the very definition of identification (Def. 17)
does not even mention the true SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}, since it is completely out of reach in most
practical situations. The task here is indeed about whether one can get by without having to know
much about the underlying collection of mechanisms and still answer the query of interest. (In
causal terminology, an identification instance is called non-parametric whenever no constraints are
imposed over the functional class ℱ\mathcal{F} or exogenous distribution P⁡(𝐔)P(\mathbf{U}).

The full causal pipeline encompassing both tasks of effect identification and estimation is
illustrated in Fig. 15. Note that the detachment with respect to the true Nature, alluded to above,
is quite prominent in the figure. The very left part contains ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}, which is
abstracted away in the form of the causal diagram 𝒢\mathcal{G} and through its marks imprinted into
the observational distribution P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V}). The symbolic methods takes the pair ⟨𝒢,P⁡(𝐕)⟩\
langle\mathcal{G},P(\mathbf{V})\rangle as input (instead of the unobservable ⟨ℱ∗,P∗​(𝐔)⟩\langle\
mathcal{F}^{*},P^{*}(\mathbf{U})\rangle), and attempt to determine whether the target distribution
QQ can be expressed in terms of the available input distributions. Formally, the question asked is
about whether there exists a mapping ff such that

 Q=f𝒢​(P​(𝐕)).\displaystyle Q=f_{\mathcal{G}}(P(\mathbf{V})).  (62)

generates input to the estimation task (2).

The decision problem regarding the existence of f(.)f(.) is certainly within the domain of causal
reasoning since it takes an arbitrary causal diagram as input, which encodes causal assumptions
about the underlying SCM, and reason through causal axioms on whether this is sufficient to perform
the intended cross-layer inference. Causal reasoning is nothing more than the manipulation and
subsequent derivation of new causal facts from known causal invariances. Whenever this step is
successfully realized, one can then ignore the causal invariances (or assumptions) entirely, and
simply

ℳ∗=⟨ℱ∗,P∗​(𝐔)⟩\mathcal{M}^{*}=\langle\mathcal{F}^{*},P^{*}(\mathbf{U})\rangle(unobserved truth)ℳ=
⟨ℱ,P⁡(𝐔)⟩\mathcal{M}=\langle\mathcal{F},P(\mathbf{U})\rangleℳ′=⟨ℱ′,P′​(𝐔)⟩\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\

SCMs thatinduce 𝒢∗\mathcal{G}^{*} and P∗​(𝐕)P^{*}(\mathbf{V})(Ω∗​(𝒢∗,P∗​(𝐕))\Omega^{*}(\mathcal{G}^
{*},P^{*}(\mathbf{V})))
Figure 16: In the case where QQ is identifiable, any two SCMs in Ω∗​(𝒢∗,P∗​(𝐕))\Omega^{*}(\mathcal
{G}^{*},P^{*}(\mathbf{V})) – the space of SCMs matching ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} in P∗​(𝐕)P^{*}(\mathbf
{V}) and 𝒢∗\mathcal{G}^{*} – will also match ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} in QQ.

try to evaluate the r.h.s. of Eq. 62 to compute QQ.

We note that in most practical settings, only a dataset with samples collected from P⁡(𝐕)P(\mathbf
{V}) is available, say D⁡(𝐕)D(\mathbf{V}), as opposed to the distribution itself. This entails the
second task that asks for a computationally and statistically attractive way of evaluating the
r.h.s. of Eq. 62 from the finite samples contained in the dataset D⁡(𝐕)D(\mathbf{V}). Even though
the l.h.s. of Eq. 62 is a causal distribution, the evaluation is complete oblivious to the
semantics of such quantity and is entirely about fitting ff using the data D⁡(𝐕)D(\mathbf{V}) in
the best possible way.

We now turn our attention to the optimization-based approach as outlined in Alg. 1 (Sec. 3) and
note that it will have a very different interpretation of the identifiability definition. In fact,
instead of avoiding the SCM altogether and focusing on the causal diagrams’ constraints, as done by
the symbolic approach, it will put the SCM at the front and center of the analysis. Fig. 16
illustrates this point by showing the space of all SCMs called Ω∗\Omega^{*} (in dark gray). The
true, unknown SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} is shown as a black dot and generates the pair ⟨𝒢∗,P∗(𝐕)\langle
\mathcal{G}^{*},P^{*}(\mathbf{V}), the latter is taken as the input of the identification analysis.
The approach will then focus on the set of SCMs that have the same interventional constraints as 𝒢∗
\mathcal{G}^{*} (Def. 5) and that also have the capability of generating the same observational
distribution P∗​(𝐕)P^{*}(\mathbf{V}) (Def. 4). This subspace is called Ω∗​(𝒢∗,P∗​(𝐕))\Omega^{*}(\
mathcal{G}^{*},P^{*}(\mathbf{V})) and marked in light gray. Since ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} is almost never
inferrable, the optimization-approach will search for two SCMs ℳ,ℳ′∈Ω∗​(𝒢∗,P∗​(𝐕))\mathcal{M},\
mathcal{M}^{\prime}\in\Omega^{*}(\mathcal{G}^{*},P^{*}(\mathbf{V})) such that the former will try
to maximize the target query (Qm​a​xQ_{max}), while the latter minimizes it (Qm​i​nQ_{min}). Two
candidate SCMs for this job are shown in the figure. Whenever the search ends and two of such SCMs
are discovered, they will predict exactly the same interventional distribution (i.e., Qm​i​n=
Qm​a​xQ_{min}=Q_{max}) if QQ is identifiable. This is because by the definition of identifiability
(Def. 17), all SCMs in the light gray area will necessarily exhibited the same interventional
behavior.

ℳ∗=⟨ℱ∗,P∗​(𝐔)⟩\mathcal{M}^{*}=\langle\mathcal{F}^{*},P^{*}(\mathbf{U})\rangle(unobserved truth)ℳ=
⟨ℱ,P⁡(𝐔)⟩\mathcal{M}=\langle\mathcal{F},P(\mathbf{U})\rangleℳ′=⟨ℱ′,P′​(𝐔)⟩\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\
langle\mathcal{F}^{\prime},P^{\prime}(\mathbf{U})\rangleSpace of All SCMs (Ω∗\Omega^{*})Space of
SCMs thatinduce 𝒢∗\mathcal{G}^{*} and P∗​(𝐕)P^{*}(\mathbf{V})(Ω∗​(𝒢∗,P∗​(𝐕))\Omega^{*}(\mathcal{G}^
{*},P^{*}(\mathbf{V})))
Figure 17: In the case where QQ is non-identifiable, there exist two SCMs in Ω∗​(𝒢∗,P∗​(𝐕))\Omega^
{*}(\mathcal{G}^{*},P^{*}(\mathbf{V})) (the space of SCMs matching ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} in P∗​(𝐕)P^{*}
(\mathbf{V}) and 𝒢∗\mathcal{G}^{*}) that do not match ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} in QQ.

On the other hand, the situation is qualitatively different when considering non-identifiable
effects. To understand how, the first observation comes from the contrapositive of Def. 17, which
says that non-identifiability implies there exists (at least) two SCMs ℳ,ℳ′\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^
{\prime} within the Ω∗​(𝒢∗,P∗​(𝐕))\Omega^{*}(\mathcal{G}^{*},P^{*}(\mathbf{V})) subspace that share
the constraints as in 𝒢∗\mathcal{G}^{*} and generate the same observational distribution (P​(𝐕)=P′​
(𝐕)P(\mathbf{V})=P^{\prime}(\mathbf{V})), but induce different interventional distributions(P⁡(Y|
d​o​(X))≠P′​(Y|d​o​(X))P(Y|do(X))\neq P^{\prime}(Y|do(X))). The optimization-based approach will
try to exploit precisely this fact, searching for non-identifiability witnesses, possibly different
than the true ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}. Those are illustrated as red dots and hollow circles in Fig. 17.
Still, this is all that is needed to determine whether an effect is not identifiable. In practice,
each distribution 𝒬,𝒬′\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime} will only be approximations and it may be
hard to detect non-identifiability depending on how close they are from each other. This is indeed
what leads to the probabilistic nature of such identifiability statements when using
optimization-based methods (as discussed in Appendix B), as opposed to the deterministic ones
entailed by the do-calculus and symbolic family.

By and large, both approaches take the causal assumptions and try to infer something about the
unknown, target quantity P∗​(Y|d​o​(X=x))P^{*}(Y|do(X=x)) that is defined by the unobserved ℳ∗\
mathcal{M}^{*}. In the case of the symbolic approach, the structural assumptions encoded in the
causal diagram 𝒢\mathcal{G} are used, while an optimization-based approach will use constraints
encoded through the scope of the functions and independence among the exogenous variables 𝐔\mathbf
{U}. Evaluating P∗​(Y∣d​o​(X=x))P^{*}(Y\mid do(X=x)) is possible in identifiable cases, which means
that our predictions will match what the true ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} would say, while the assumptions
would be too weak in others cases, and non-identifiability will take place, which means we are
unable to make any statement about ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*} without strengthening the assumptions.

After having understood the different takes of both approaches to the problem of identification, we
consider again the entire pipeline shown in Fig. 15. We note that the two tasks, identification and
estimation, are both necessary in any causal analysis, but they are usually studied separately in
the symbolic literature. Symbolic methods returns the identifiability status of a query and include
the mapping ff whenever the effect is identifiable. The target quantity can then be evaluated by
standard statistical methods (plug-in) or more refined learning procedures, e.g., multi-propensity
score/inverse probability weighting Jung et al., 2020a (), empirical risk minimization Jung et al.,
2020b (), and double machine learning Jung et al., (2021).

On the other hand, the optimization-based framework proposed here is capable of identifying and
estimating the target quantities in an integrated manner. As shown through simulations (Sec. 5),
given the sampling nature of the optimization procedure, the performance of the method relies not
only on the causal assumptions, but also on accurate optimization, which may require a large amount
of samples and computation for training.

In practice, one may consider a hybrid approach where a symbolic algorithm for the identification
step is ran first, since it is deterministic and always returns the right answer, and then the
estimation step is performed through an NCM. With this use case in mind, we define Alg. 4 in Fig.
18 (left) as this hybrid alternative to the more pure Alg. 1. In some sense, this approach combines
the best of both worlds – it relies on an ideal ID algorithm, which is correct with probability
one, and the powerful computational properties of neural networks, which may perform well and scale
to complex settings, for estimation. We illustrate this using the two figures in Fig. 18 (right).

Figure 18: (Left panel) Algorithm for solving identification problem with symbolic solvers and
estimation with NCMs. (Right) Schematic illustrating differences between Alg. 1 (a) and Alg. 4 (b).

After all, the NCM approach provides a cohesive framework that unifies both the causal and the
statistical components involved in evaluating interventional distributions. In the case of the
hybrid approach, we note that the causal reasoning is shifted to the symbolic identification
methods. It is remarkable but somewhat unsurprising that neural methods, which are the
state-of-the-art for function approximations, are capable of performing the statistical step
required for causal estimation. More interestingly, we demonstrate in this work that neural nets
(and proxy SCMs, in general) are also capable of performing causal reasoning, such as in the
inferences required to solve the ID problem. Many other works have studied using neural networks as
an estimation tool, as listed in the introduction, but to the best of our knowledge, our work is
the first one that utilizes neural networks to provide a complete solution within the neural
framework to the identification problem, and causal reasoning more broadly.

In addition to showing that neural nets are also capable of performing causal reasoning, there are
other implications for using a proxy SCM in place of the true SCM, as opposed to abstracting the
true SCM entirely. Firstly, the generative capabilities of proxy SCMs, like the NCM, can be useful
if the user desires a source of infinite data, which is a quite common use case found in the
literature. Secondly, it provides quick estimation results for multiple queries via the mutilation
procedure without the need for retraining. Whenever we apply a symbolic approach we need to derive
(and then train) a specialized estimand, which can be time consuming. Thirdly, working in the space
of SCMs tends to provide straightforward interpretation of the causal problems using its semantics,
which implies that it has the potential to be more easily extensible to other related problems. One
may be interested in generalizations of the ID problem such as identifying other types of queries
or identifying and fusing from different sources and experimental conditions (Bareinboim et al.,,
2012; Bareinboim et al.,, 2015; Lee et al.,, 2019), such as in reinforcement learning. In general,
extending the line of reasoning of Alg. 1 to other identification cases is much simpler than
deriving a new symbolic solution under different constraints.

Appendix D Generalizations

D.1 NCMs with other Functions and Noise Distributions (Proofs)

For the sake of concreteness and simplicity of exposition, the NCM from Def. 3 specifically uses
Unif⁡(0,1)\unif(0,1) noise for the variables in 𝐔\mathbf{U} and MLPs as functions. This leads to
easy implementation and concrete examples for discussion (as in Appendix C).

We note that these are not meant to be limitations for the NCM framework. The NCM object can be
extended to use other function or noise types, provided they exhibit certain properties. We will
henceforth refer to specific versions of the NCM with their particular implementation of the noise
and functions (e.g., Def. 3 will be called NCM-FF-Unif).

Consider a more general version of the NCM below:

18NCM (General Case).

A Neural Causal Model (for short, NCM) M^​(𝛉)\widehat{M}(\bm{\theta}) over variables 𝐕\mathbf{V}
with parameters 𝛉={θVi:Vi∈𝐕}\bm{\theta}=\{\theta_{V_{i}}:V_{i}\in\mathbf{V}\} is an SCM ⟨𝐔^,𝐕,ℱ^,P^
​(𝐔^)⟩\langle\widehat{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{V},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{P}(\widehat{\mathbf
{U}})\rangle such that

  • •
   
    𝐔^⊆{U^𝐂:𝐂⊆𝐕}\widehat{\mathbf{U}}\subseteq\{\widehat{U}_{\mathbf{C}}:\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf
    {V}\}, where each U^\widehat{U} is associated with some subset of variables 𝐂⊆𝐕\mathbf{C}\
    subseteq\mathbf{V};

  • •

    {f}_{V_{i}}, parameterized by θVi∈𝜽\theta_{V_{i}}\in\bm{\theta}, maps values of 𝐔Vi∪𝐏𝐚Vi\mathbf
    {U}_{V_{i}}\cup\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{i}} to values of ViV_{i} for some 𝐏𝐚Vi⊆𝐕\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{i}}\
    subseteq\mathbf{V} and 𝐔Vi={U^𝐂:U^𝐂∈𝐔^,Vi∈𝐂}\mathbf{U}_{V_{i}}=\{\widehat{U}_{\mathbf{C}}:\
    widehat{U}_{\mathbf{C}}\in\widehat{\mathbf{U}},V_{i}\in\mathbf{C}\};

Furthermore, a NCM M^​(𝛉)\widehat{M}(\bm{\theta}) should have the following properties:
(P1) For all U^∈𝐔^\widehat{U}\in\widehat{\mathbf{U}}, U^\widehat{U} has a well-defined probability
density function P⁡(U^)P(\widehat{U}) (i.e. its cumulative density function is absolutely
continuous).
(P2) For all functions f^Vi∈ℱ^\hat{f}_{V_{i}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}} and all functions g:ℝ→𝕊g:\
mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}, where 𝕊⊂ℝ\mathbb{S}\subset\mathbb{R} is a countable set, there
exists parameterization θVi∗\theta^{*}_{V_{i}} such that f^Vi​(⋅,θVi∗)=g\hat{f}_{V_{i}}(\cdot;\
theta^{*}_{V_{i}})=g (universal representation). ■\blacksquare

We can use these properties to prove a more general version of Thm. 1. We first state the following
result to aid the proof.

5Probability Integral Transform (Casella and Berger,, 2001, Thm. 2.1.10).

For any random variable XX with a well-defined probability density function P⁡(X)P(X), there exists
a function f:𝒟X→[0,1]f:\mathcal{D}_{X}\rightarrow[0,1] such that f⁡(X)f(X) is a Unif⁡(0,1)\unif
(0,1) random variable. ■\blacksquare

The generalized proof follows.

5NCM Expressiveness.

For any SCM ℳ∗=⟨𝐔,𝐕,ℱ,P⁡(𝐔)⟩\mathcal{M}^{*}=\langle\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V},\mathcal{F},P(\mathbf{U})\
rangle, there exists a NCM M^​(𝛉)=⟨𝐔^,𝐕,ℱ^,P^​(𝐔^)⟩\widehat{M}(\bm{\theta})=\langle\widehat{\mathbf
{U}},\mathbf{V},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{P}(\widehat{\mathbf{U}})\rangle s.t. M^\widehat{M}
is L3L_{3}-consistent w.r.t. ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}. ■\blacksquare

Proof.

Lemma 2 guarantees that there exists a canonical SCM ℳCM=⟨𝐔CM,𝐕,ℱCM,P⁡(𝐔CM)⟩\mathcal{M}_{\ctm}=\
langle\mathbf{U}_{\ctm},\mathbf{V},\mathcal{F}_{\ctm},P(\mathbf{U}_{\ctm})\rangle that is L3L_{3}
-consistent with ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}. Hence, to construct M^\widehat{M}, it suffices to show how to
construct ℳCM\mathcal{M}_{\ctm} using the architecture of a NCM.

Following Def. 18, we choose 𝐔^={U^𝐕}\widehat{\mathbf{U}}=\{\widehat{U}_{\mathbf{V}}\}. By property
P1 and Fact 5, there exists a function gUg^{U} such that gU​(U^𝐕)g^{U}(\widehat{U}_{\mathbf{V}}) is
a Unif⁡(0,1)\unif(0,1) random variable.

Using the construction in Lem. 5, there must also exist a function gRg^{R} such that

 P⁡(gR​(gU​(U𝐕^)))=P⁡(𝐔CM)P(g^{R}(g^{U}(\widehat{U_{\mathbf{V}}})))=P(\mathbf{U}_{\ctm})  (63)

{\mathcal{F}}. Combining the above results, there must exist

 gVi=fViCM​(𝐩𝐚Vi,gR​(gU​(U^𝐕)))g_{V_{i}}=f_{V_{i}}^{\ctm}\left(\mathbf{pa}_{V_{i}},g^{R}\left  (64)
 (g^{U}(\widehat{U}_{\mathbf{V}})\right)\right)

hat{f}_{V_{i}}(\cdot;\theta^{*}_{V_{i}})=g_{V_{i}}.

By Eqs. 63 and 64, the NCM M^\widehat{M} is constructed to match ℳCM\mathcal{M}_{\ctm} on all
outputs. Hence, for any counterfactual query 𝛗\bm{\upvarphi}, we have

 ℳCM⊧𝛗⇔M^⊧𝛗\mathcal{M}_{\ctm}\models\bm{\upvarphi}\Leftrightarrow\widehat{M}\models\bm{\upvarphi}

and therefore

∎

The general 𝒢\mathcal{G}-constrained version of the NCM follows the same procedure as Def. 7.

19𝒢\mathcal{G}-Constrained NCM (General Case).

Let 𝒢\mathcal{G} be the causal diagram induced by SCM ℳ∗\mathcal{M}^{*}. Construct NCM M^\widehat
{M} as follows. (1) Choose 𝐔^\widehat{\mathbf{U}} s.t. U^𝐂∈𝐔^\widehat{U}_{\mathbf{C}}\in\widehat{\
mathbf{U}} if and only if 𝐂\mathbf{C} is a C2C^{2}-component in 𝒢\mathcal{G}. Each U^𝐂\widehat{U}_
{\mathbf{C}} has its own distribution P⁡(U^𝐂)P(\widehat{U}_{\mathbf{C}}) independent of other
variables in 𝐔^\widehat{\mathbf{U}}, but it is shared as input to the functions of all variables in
𝐂\mathbf{C}. (2) For each variable Vi∈𝐕V_{i}\in\mathbf{V}, choose 𝐏𝐚Vi⊆𝐕\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{i}}\
subseteq\mathbf{V} s.t. for every Vj∈𝐕V_{j}\in\mathbf{V}, Vj∈𝐏𝐚ViV_{j}\in\mathbf{Pa}_{V_{i}} if and
only if there is a directed edge from VjV_{j} to ViV_{i} in 𝒢\mathcal{G}. Any NCM in this family is
said to be 𝒢\mathcal{G}-constrained. ■\blacksquare

We prove a general version of Thm. 3 similarly.

6NCM L2L_{2}-𝒢\mathcal{G} Representation.

{U}_{\mathbf{C}}:\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{C}(\mathcal{G})\text{ s.t. }V\in\mathbf{C}\}.

 prime}_{V})

for each V∈𝐕V\in\mathbf{V}.

To choose the functions of ℱ^\widehat{\mathcal{F}}, consider each f^Vi∈ℱ^\hat{f}_{V_{i}}\in\widehat
{\mathcal{F}}. Combining the above results, there must exist

 gVi=fVi′​(𝐩𝐚Vi,gViR​(gViU​(𝐔^V)))g_{V_{i}}=f_{V_{i}}^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{pa}_{V_{i}},g_{V_  (66)

By Eqs. 65 and 66, the NCM M^\widehat{M} is constructed to match ℳ′\mathcal{M}^{\prime} on all
